### Benchmarking Federated Learning for Throughput Prediction in 5G Live Streaming Applications
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08479v1)
- **Authors**: Yuvraj Dutta, Soumyajit Chatterjee, Sandip Chakraborty, Basabdatta Palit
- **Abstract**: Accurate and adaptive network throughput prediction is essential for latency-sensitive and bandwidth-intensive applications in 5G and emerging 6G networks. However, most existing methods rely on centralized training with uniformly collected data, limiting their applicability in heterogeneous mobile environments with non-IID data distributions. This paper presents the first comprehensive benchmarking of federated learning (FL) strategies for throughput prediction in realistic 5G edge scenarios. We evaluate three aggregation algorithms - FedAvg, FedProx, and FedBN - across four time-series architectures: LSTM, CNN, CNN+LSTM, and Transformer, using five diverse real-world datasets. We systematically analyze the effects of client heterogeneity, cohort size, and history window length on prediction performance. Our results reveal key trade-offs among model complexities, convergence rates, and generalization. It is found that FedBN consistently delivers robust performance under non-IID conditions. On the other hand, LSTM and Transformer models outperform CNN-based baselines by up to 80% in R2 scores. Moreover, although Transformers converge in half the rounds of LSTM, they require longer history windows to achieve a high R2, indicating higher context dependence. LSTM is, therefore, found to achieve a favorable balance between accuracy, rounds, and temporal footprint. To validate the end-to-end applicability of the framework, we have integrated our FL-based predictors into a live adaptive streaming pipeline. It is seen that FedBN-based LSTM and Transformer models improve mean QoE scores by 11.7% and 11.4%, respectively, over FedAvg, while also reducing the variance. These findings offer actionable insights for building scalable, privacy-preserving, and edge-aware throughput prediction systems in next-generation wireless networks.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a benchmarking study of Federated Learning (FL) strategies for network throughput prediction in 5G live streaming applications. It comprehensively evaluates different FL aggregation algorithms (FedAvg, FedProx, FedBN) across various time-series model architectures (LSTM, CNN, CNN+LSTM, and Transformer) using real-world 5G datasets. The study analyzes the impact of client heterogeneity, cohort size, and history window length on prediction performance. The findings reveal trade-offs between model complexity, convergence rates, and generalization capabilities.  The paper validates the practical applicability of the framework by integrating FL-based predictors into a live adaptive streaming pipeline, demonstrating improved Quality of Experience (QoE) using FedBN-based LSTM and Transformer models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

The paper makes a solid contribution by providing a systematic and comprehensive benchmark of FL strategies for throughput prediction in 5G networks.  While the application of FL to throughput prediction isn't entirely novel (the authors acknowledge their previous work, FedPut), this work goes significantly beyond existing literature in several important ways:

*   **Comprehensive Benchmarking:** The paper's strength lies in its systematic evaluation of various FL algorithms and model architectures, offering a wider perspective than previous studies that focused on specific combinations. This extensive analysis fills a gap in the existing literature and provides valuable insights for researchers and practitioners.
*   **Real-world Datasets and Heterogeneity Analysis:** The use of multiple, diverse real-world 5G datasets significantly enhances the practical relevance of the findings. The detailed analysis of the impact of non-IID data distributions, cohort composition, and client diversity is crucial for understanding the challenges of deploying FL in heterogeneous mobile edge environments.
*   **Practical Validation in Live Streaming:** Integrating the FL-based predictors into a live adaptive streaming pipeline demonstrates the end-to-end utility of the framework and quantifies its impact on user-perceived QoE. This practical validation adds significant value to the research, showcasing the real-world benefits of FL for throughput prediction.
*   **Actionable Insights:** The paper identifies FedBN as a robust choice for non-IID settings and highlights the trade-offs between LSTM and Transformer models in terms of accuracy, convergence speed, and temporal footprint. These findings provide actionable insights for building scalable and privacy-preserving throughput prediction systems.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Novelty of Individual Components:** While the comprehensive benchmarking is a significant contribution, the individual FL algorithms and model architectures themselves are well-established.  The paper's novelty primarily resides in their application, combination, and systematic evaluation in the context of 5G throughput prediction.
*   **Simulation-based Evaluation:** The live streaming evaluation is still based on simulations using publicly available datasets.  A real-world deployment with actual mobile devices and network conditions would further strengthen the results and provide a more realistic assessment of the framework's performance.
*   **Limited Exploration of Alternative FL Techniques:** While FedAvg, FedProx, and FedBN are important baselines, the paper could benefit from exploring other advanced FL techniques such as federated distillation or personalized FL to address client heterogeneity more effectively.

**Significance:**

The paper's findings are significant for several reasons:

*   It addresses a critical challenge in 5G and emerging 6G networks: accurate and adaptive network throughput prediction for latency-sensitive applications.
*   It provides a comprehensive benchmark for evaluating FL strategies in heterogeneous mobile edge environments, offering valuable guidance for researchers and practitioners.
*   It demonstrates the practical benefits of FL for improving user-perceived QoE in live adaptive streaming applications.
*   It promotes the development of scalable and privacy-preserving throughput prediction systems, addressing the limitations of centralized training approaches.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper makes a significant contribution to the field. While the individual components (FL algorithms, model architectures) are not entirely novel, their systematic evaluation, combination, and practical validation in a relevant application domain (5G live streaming) is a valuable and timely contribution. The rigorous analysis of heterogeneity and the actionable insights provided further enhance the paper's significance. The main limitation is the lack of real-world testbed deployment.

**Score: 7.5**

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### MuGa-VTON: Multi-Garment Virtual Try-On via Diffusion Transformers with Prompt Customization
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08488v1)
- **Authors**: Ankan Deria, Dwarikanath Mahapatra, Behzad Bozorgtabar, Mohna Chakraborty, Snehashis Chakraborty, Sudipta Roy
- **Abstract**: Virtual try-on seeks to generate photorealistic images of individuals in desired garments, a task that must simultaneously preserve personal identity and garment fidelity for practical use in fashion retail and personalization. However, existing methods typically handle upper and lower garments separately, rely on heavy preprocessing, and often fail to preserve person-specific cues such as tattoos, accessories, and body shape-resulting in limited realism and flexibility. To this end, we introduce MuGa-VTON, a unified multi-garment diffusion framework that jointly models upper and lower garments together with person identity in a shared latent space. Specifically, we proposed three key modules: the Garment Representation Module (GRM) for capturing both garment semantics, the Person Representation Module (PRM) for encoding identity and pose cues, and the A-DiT fusion module, which integrates garment, person, and text-prompt features through a diffusion transformer. This architecture supports prompt-based customization, allowing fine-grained garment modifications with minimal user input. Extensive experiments on the VITON-HD and DressCode benchmarks demonstrate that MuGa-VTON outperforms existing methods in both qualitative and quantitative evaluations, producing high-fidelity, identity-preserving results suitable for real-world virtual try-on applications.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the MuGa-VTON paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MuGa-VTON, a new framework for multi-garment virtual try-on with prompt customization.  It uses diffusion transformers to jointly model upper and lower garments, along with person identity, in a shared latent space. The framework consists of three core modules: a Garment Representation Module (GRM), a Person Representation Module (PRM), and an A-DiT fusion module.  The A-DiT module integrates garment, person, and text-prompt features through a diffusion transformer, enabling prompt-based edits for fine-grained garment modifications. The authors demonstrate through experiments that MuGa-VTON outperforms existing methods in realism, identity preservation, and customization capabilities.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel components. The unified framework for jointly modeling upper and lower garments addresses a key limitation of previous approaches that handle garments separately. The use of diffusion transformers, especially the proposed A-DiT module, is a valuable architectural contribution. The prompt-based customization for fine-grained editing is a significant enhancement over existing methods and allows for greater user control. The progressive training strategy for handling resolution is also a solid contribution. However, using diffusion models for VTON, while incorporating transformer architecture, is not entirely new in itself; several existing VTON papers also use similar approaches. The novelty comes from the specific combination of modules and the integration of prompt engineering for garment editing.

*   **Significance:** The ability to generate realistic virtual try-on results with fine-grained customization has significant practical implications. If successfully implemented, MuGa-VTON could improve user experiences in online fashion retail and personalization, enabling users to create complete and customized outfits. The preservation of identity-specific attributes (e.g., tattoos, accessories) is also a key aspect that enhances the realism and utility of the system. The demonstrated performance gains over existing methods, both qualitatively and quantitatively, suggest that MuGa-VTON represents a significant advancement in the field. However, the limitations regarding complex logos and textures needs further improvement to achieve higher realism and practicality.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   Unified multi-garment handling.
    *   Prompt-based customization for fine-grained editing.
    *   Preservation of identity-specific attributes.
    *   Strong quantitative and qualitative results.
    *   Adoption of a progressive training strategy for high fidelity
    *   Use of high-quality segmentation masks from Sapiens to better preserve identity.
    *   Structured prompt generation using GPT-4 to enable fine-grained control

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   Limitations with complex logos and textures.
    *   Difficulty with uncommon clothing combinations.
    *   Dependence on powerful foundation models such as diffusion models and GPT for prompting might hinder the model from being accessible for users with limited computational resources.
    *   The results shown can exhibit some warping artifacts.

*   **Potential Influence:** MuGa-VTON has the potential to influence future research in virtual try-on by demonstrating the effectiveness of diffusion transformers for multi-garment modeling and prompt-based customization. The framework's modular design could also inspire new architectures and training strategies for other generative tasks.

*   **Score:** 7.5

**Rationale for Score:**

MuGa-VTON makes a significant contribution to the field of virtual try-on by addressing key limitations of existing methods and introducing innovative features like prompt-based customization and unified multi-garment handling. The strong performance gains and the demonstrated preservation of identity attributes make it a valuable advancement. The modular framework also presents an avenue for future research. However, the limitations regarding logo reproduction, uncommon combinations, and dependence on powerful foundational models hold it back from achieving a higher score. There is also existing work that uses diffusion models in the area of VTON; the paper does not *revolutionize* the methodology, but is certainly an *evolution* of the state of the art. The novelty lies in the architecture and training pipeline, rather than a brand-new paradigm. Therefore, a score of 7.5 seems appropriate, balancing the strengths with the current limitations and placing the paper as a solid, impactful contribution to the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### Momentum Point-Perplexity Mechanics in Large Language Models
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08492v1)
- **Authors**: Lorenzo Tomaz, Judd Rosenblatt, Thomas Berry Jones, Diogo Schwerz de Lucena
- **Abstract**: We take a physics-based approach to studying how the internal hidden states of large language models change from token to token during inference. Across 20 open-source transformer models (135M-3B parameters), we find that a quantity combining the rate of change in hidden states and the model's next-token certainty, analogous to energy in physics, remains nearly constant. Random-weight models conserve this "energy" more tightly than pre-trained ones, while training shifts models into a faster, more decisive regime with greater variability. Using this "log-Lagrangian" view, we derive a control method called Jacobian steering, which perturbs hidden states in the minimal way needed to favor a target token. This approach maintained near-constant energy in two tested models and produced continuations rated higher in semantic quality than the models' natural outputs. Viewing transformers through this mechanics lens offers a principled basis for interpretability, anomaly detection, and low-risk steering. This could help make powerful models more predictable and aligned with human intent.
- **Summary**: Okay, here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a physics-inspired approach to analyze and control the dynamics of transformer language models. It proposes a "log-Lagrangian" framework, combining hidden-state velocities and point perplexity, and argues that this quantity is approximately conserved during inference, analogous to energy conservation in physical systems. The authors empirically validate this conservation law across various transformer models (135M-3B parameters), showing it's particularly tight in random-weight models. Furthermore, the paper demonstrates how Jacobian steering, a technique for controlling model outputs by perturbing hidden states, arises naturally from this variational framework as a minimal-action intervention. The steered models show improved semantic quality compared to their natural outputs. The paper concludes by suggesting that this mechanics lens offers a principled basis for interpretability, anomaly detection, and low-risk steering in language models.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of applying a Lagrangian mechanics framework to understand transformer dynamics is somewhat novel, but it builds upon existing lines of research, including work on dynamical systems interpretations of deep learning and mechanistic interpretability. It's not a completely groundbreaking idea, but the specific formulation using log-Lagrangians and the empirical validation are valuable. The connection of logit changes to hidden state velocity is less novel, building on existing logit lens work.
* **Significance:** The potential significance is substantial but contingent on future development. If this framework can indeed lead to more robust control mechanisms, better anomaly detection, and more interpretable models, it would be a valuable contribution to the field of AI safety and interpretability. The demonstrated improvement in semantic quality via Jacobian steering, guided by the framework, is a promising initial result. However, more extensive testing and comparison with alternative steering methods are necessary. A deeper understanding of why the energy is conserved and how it relates to model generalization would also increase the significance. The link to physics-informed AI Safety is speculative but interesting.
* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical validation:** The paper provides empirical evidence supporting the energy conservation hypothesis across a range of models.
    * **Principled approach:** The variational framework provides a more principled basis for understanding and controlling transformer behavior compared to ad-hoc methods.
    * **Clear connection to steering:** The derivation of Jacobian steering from the minimal-action principle is elegant and provides a theoretical justification for this technique.
    * **Improved semantic quality:** The steering method, in initial tests, shows improved quality, a strong selling point.
* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Limited control evaluation:** The evaluation of the Jacobian steering method is limited in scope. More extensive testing with different tasks and comparison to other steering methods is needed.
    * **Justification of log scale**: The authors provide a good reason for using the log scale due to scale disparity issues with numerical stability, however the authors should also note the limitations of this approach, as it might not be the optimal approach and could lead to certain constraints and other limitations as well.
    * **Limited theoretical depth:** While the paper presents a theoretical framework, it lacks deeper theoretical analysis. For example, a more thorough explanation of why transformers conserve this specific quantity would strengthen the results. There should also be discussion surrounding the limitations of the framework
    * **Generality concerns:** The results are primarily demonstrated on a specific set of models and tasks. The generality of the findings to other architectures and modalities remains to be seen.
    * **Speculative Claims:** Some of the claims regarding AI safety and the potential for new architectures are speculative and require further investigation.
* **Overall:** The paper presents a promising approach to understanding and controlling transformer dynamics. The initial empirical results are encouraging, and the theoretical framework provides a potentially valuable lens for future research. However, the paper is not without its limitations. The control mechanisms are limited and would require extensive testing. The log formulation should also be heavily scrutinized and would require more justification. The claim that this is a principled method for controlling neural language models would need to be justified, and the speculation of physics-inspired AI Safety would need to be examined.

**Score: 6**

*Rationale:* The paper presents a moderately novel and potentially significant approach to understanding transformer dynamics. The empirical validation is a strength, but the lack of comprehensive evaluation of the control mechanisms, limited theoretical depth, and some speculative claims hold it back. While the theoretical justification is good, some improvements should be considered (see above). The work shows promise but requires further development to realize its full potential.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 6/10

### Large Language Models as Oracles for Ontology Alignment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08500v1)
- **Authors**: Sviatoslav Lushnei, Dmytro Shumskyi, Severyn Shykula, Ernesto Jimenez-Ruiz, Artur d'Avila Garcez
- **Abstract**: Ontology alignment plays a crucial role in integrating diverse data sources across domains. There is a large plethora of systems that tackle the ontology alignment problem, yet challenges persist in producing highly quality correspondences among a set of input ontologies. Human-in-the-loop during the alignment process is essential in applications requiring very accurate mappings. User involvement is, however, expensive when dealing with large ontologies. In this paper, we explore the feasibility of using Large Language Models (LLM) as an alternative to the domain expert. The use of the LLM focuses only on the validation of the subset of correspondences where an ontology alignment system is very uncertain. We have conducted an extensive evaluation over several matching tasks of the Ontology Alignment Evaluation Initiative (OAEI), analysing the performance of several state-of-the-art LLMs using different ontology-driven prompt templates. The LLM results are also compared against simulated Oracles with variable error rates.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores the use of Large Language Models (LLMs) as "Oracles" to improve ontology alignment, specifically by focusing on validating uncertain correspondences identified by the LogMap system. It aims to reduce the need for costly human-in-the-loop validation by leveraging LLMs' knowledge to assess mappings where traditional alignment techniques are insufficient. The authors evaluate various LLMs (GPT-4o Mini and several Gemini Flash models) using different ontology-driven prompts, considering lexical, contextual, and synonym information. They assess the LLMs' diagnostic capabilities, their contribution to overall alignment performance (compared to LogMap and simulated oracles), and the impact of different prompt templates. The evaluation is conducted on multiple OAEI datasets.  The core idea is a cost-effective architecture where LLMs are only used in specific, challenging cases to minimize costs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies in its specific approach to integrating LLMs into ontology alignment: using them as validation oracles for uncertain mappings identified by a traditional system (LogMap). While other work has explored LLMs for alignment, this targeted approach aims for cost-effectiveness and reduced reliance on LLMs. The detailed exploration of different prompt templates with varying levels of contextual and lexical information is also a strength.  A less significant aspect is the assessment of multiple Gemini variants, although this could have limited long-term relevance due to LLM evolution.

*   **Significance:** The significance of this work lies in its potential to make LLM-enhanced ontology alignment more accessible. By minimizing LLM usage, the authors address the financial and infrastructural barriers often associated with LLM-based solutions.  A successful oracle system could significantly reduce the human effort required for high-precision ontology alignment. Comparing against simulated oracles provides a crucial benchmark, indicating where LLMs may perform as well or even better than a domain expert (with some degree of error).

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Targeted LLM usage:** Focuses LLM on the most difficult tasks, optimizing for cost.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** Thoroughly evaluates multiple LLMs, prompt templates, and datasets.
    *   **Comparison with baselines:**  Compares results against LogMap, and simulated oracles, allowing benchmarking.
    *   **Analysis of Prompt Engineering:** In-depth investigation of the impact of different prompts and context.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited LLM Scope:** The results are inherently limited by the current capabilities of the tested LLMs.  Rapid advancements in LLM technology mean these results might not be fully generalizable in the future.
    *   **Dependence on LogMap:** The system relies on LogMap to identify the uncertain mappings, introducing a dependence and potential bias.  The quality of LogMap's uncertainty estimations directly impacts the quality and relevance of prompts sent to the LLM oracle.
    *   **Training Data Leakage Concerns:** Acknowledges the potential for LLMs to have been pre-trained on the OAEI datasets, potentially skewing the results.
    *  **Generality:**  The paper is very focused on a specific domain (biomedical ontologies).  The prompts and models used may not translate effectively to other domains with different linguistic or structural characteristics.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the design of more practical and cost-effective ontology alignment systems. The insights into prompt engineering and LLM diagnostic capabilities could be valuable for future research. However, the reliance on proprietary LLMs and the potential for training data leakage limit the immediate impact. The success of the system relies heavily on the performance of the underlying automatic aligner and how well its uncertainty correlates with cases where LLMs can provide useful validation.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a valuable and well-executed exploration of using LLMs as targeted oracles for ontology alignment. While not groundbreaking, the work contributes meaningfully to the field by addressing the practical limitations of LLM-based solutions. The detailed evaluation and the comparison with simulated oracles are particularly strong aspects. However, the limitations related to dependence on LogMap, the rapidly evolving LLM landscape, and the potential for training data leakage prevent a higher score.

**Score: 6.5**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 6/10

### GVGAI-LLM: Evaluating Large Language Model Agents with Infinite Games
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08501v1)
- **Authors**: Yuchen Li, Cong Lin, Muhammad Umair Nasir, Philip Bontrager, Jialin Liu, Julian Togelius
- **Abstract**: We introduce GVGAI-LLM, a video game benchmark for evaluating the reasoning and problem-solving capabilities of large language models (LLMs). Built on the General Video Game AI framework, it features a diverse collection of arcade-style games designed to test a model's ability to handle tasks that differ from most existing LLM benchmarks. The benchmark leverages a game description language that enables rapid creation of new games and levels, helping to prevent overfitting over time. Each game scene is represented by a compact set of ASCII characters, allowing for efficient processing by language models. GVGAI-LLM defines interpretable metrics, including the meaningful step ratio, step efficiency, and overall score, to assess model behavior. Through zero-shot evaluations across a broad set of games and levels with diverse challenges and skill depth, we reveal persistent limitations of LLMs in spatial reasoning and basic planning. Current models consistently exhibit spatial and logical errors, motivating structured prompting and spatial grounding techniques. While these interventions lead to partial improvements, the benchmark remains very far from solved. GVGAI-LLM provides a reproducible testbed for advancing research on language model capabilities, with a particular emphasis on agentic behavior and contextual reasoning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the GVGAI-LLM paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces GVGAI-LLM, a new benchmark for evaluating the reasoning and problem-solving abilities of large language models (LLMs) in video games.  Built upon the existing General Video Game AI (GVGAI) framework, it presents a diverse set of arcade-style games with the goal of testing LLMs in environments that demand spatial reasoning, rule understanding, and real-time decision-making. The framework includes tools for converting game states and rules into natural language prompts, and it defines metrics like meaningful step ratio and step efficiency to assess agent behavior.  The authors perform zero-shot evaluations of several LLMs, revealing limitations in spatial reasoning and planning and motivating structured prompting techniques.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the *application* of LLMs to the GVGAI framework and the creation of a standardized prompting and evaluation environment for this specific context. The idea of using games as benchmarks for LLMs isn't entirely new, but the GVGAI framework provides a unique environment with its formal game descriptions, diverse game dynamics, and potential for infinite extensibility. The paper's contribution of providing a way to directly compare LLMs to classical AI agents (like tree search and RL) in the same symbolic space is a strong point.  The developed metrics, especially the meaningful step ratio, are useful for analyzing LLM agent behavior.

**Significance:** The significance comes from addressing a gap in existing LLM benchmarks. Most focus on static language understanding, code generation, or instruction following. GVGAI-LLM offers a structured, symbolic environment that specifically tests spatial reasoning, rule-based dynamics, and reactive decision-making – all crucial for agentic behavior.  The paper clearly highlights the challenges LLMs face in these environments, particularly in spatial grounding and symbolic identity tracking. It points towards areas needing further research, like more effective structured prompting and methods for imbuing LLMs with robust planning capabilities. The framework's potential to generate new games/levels to prevent overfitting is also a significant advantage.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined benchmark environment with clear motivation.
*   Useful set of evaluation metrics capturing different aspects of agent behavior.
*   Systematic evaluation revealing the shortcomings of current LLMs in spatial reasoning and planning.
*   Framework allows direct comparison with traditional AI agents.
*   Addresses an important gap in LLM benchmark development, moving toward more agentic skills.
*   Emphasis on zero-shot learning highlights generalization ability, though it leads to high resource consumption due to prompting at each step without prior information.

**Weaknesses:**

*   The reliance on ASCII representation of game states, while efficient, might limit the complexity of scenarios that can be presented and abstract a critical element of processing real-world visual information.
*   The zero-shot approach, while promoting generalization, might not be the most effective way to elicit the best performance from LLMs. Fine-tuning or few-shot learning could potentially lead to better results, although it might diminish the benchmark's ability to prevent overfitting.
*   While the paper mentions contextual prompting, its limited exploration and the associated drawbacks raise questions about its feasibility and efficacy.
*   The paper shows relatively modest gains by the two added prompt modifications. The results show a minimal difference and could be better supported by a broader evaluation.
*   The conclusions, while sound, are based on a relatively limited number of games and LLM models.

**Potential Influence:**

The GVGAI-LLM benchmark can be influential in guiding future research on LLM agents, especially in the areas of:

*   Developing better spatial reasoning and planning capabilities for LLMs.
*   Designing more effective prompting strategies for structured environments.
*   Creating LLMs that can maintain symbolic identities across transformations.
*   Benchmarking the performance of different LLM architectures and training methods in agentic tasks.

**Rigorous Rationale for Assigned Score:**

The paper presents a solid contribution to the field by introducing a relevant benchmark and carefully analyzing the performance of LLMs. However, there are limitations in the prompt design, experimental setup, and scope of the evaluation.

Score: 7

The paper has a good degree of novelty and has a chance to impact the development of LLMs. The findings offer valuable insights into the strengths and weaknesses of existing LLMs as game-playing agents. These weaknesses may be mitigated through further research, leading to improved strategies for prompt design, enabling greater LLM effectiveness as agents.

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### When the Domain Expert Has No Time and the LLM Developer Has No Clinical Expertise: Real-World Lessons from LLM Co-Design in a Safety-Net Hospital
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08504v1)
- **Authors**: Avni Kothari, Patrick Vossler, Jean Digitale, Mohammad Forouzannia, Elise Rosenberg, Michele Lee, Jennee Bryant, Melanie Molina, James Marks, Lucas Zier, Jean Feng
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have the potential to address social and behavioral determinants of health by transforming labor intensive workflows in resource-constrained settings. Creating LLM-based applications that serve the needs of underserved communities requires a deep understanding of their local context, but it is often the case that neither LLMs nor their developers possess this local expertise, and the experts in these communities often face severe time/resource constraints. This creates a disconnect: how can one engage in meaningful co-design of an LLM-based application for an under-resourced community when the communication channel between the LLM developer and domain expert is constrained? We explored this question through a real-world case study, in which our data science team sought to partner with social workers at a safety net hospital to build an LLM application that summarizes patients' social needs. Whereas prior works focus on the challenge of prompt tuning, we found that the most critical challenge in this setting is the careful and precise specification of \what information to surface to providers so that the LLM application is accurate, comprehensive, and verifiable. Here we present a novel co-design framework for settings with limited access to domain experts, in which the summary generation task is first decomposed into individually-optimizable attributes and then each attribute is efficiently refined and validated through a multi-tier cascading approach.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the challenge of co-designing Large Language Model (LLM) applications in resource-constrained settings, specifically within a safety-net hospital. The authors highlight the difficulties arising from limited access to domain experts (social workers) and the lack of clinical expertise among LLM developers. They propose a novel, multi-tier cascading framework that decomposes the summarization task into manageable attributes, iteratively refines the definition of these attributes through bootstrapping from existing organizational artifacts and targeted feedback from cross-functional experts (CFEs) and social workers, and employs a multi-tier validation approach, incorporating both LLM-as-a-judge and human expert evaluation. The paper describes a real-world case study where this framework was applied to build an LLM application to assist social workers in summarizing patients' social needs, demonstrating its effectiveness in improving accuracy and overall usability.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers a practical solution to a genuine challenge: bridging the expertise and resource gap in LLM application development for underserved communities. The multi-tier cascading framework is a valuable contribution, particularly the idea of decomposing a complex summarization task into modular, individually optimizable attributes. The use of LLM-as-a-judge as a cost-effective initial validation step is clever. While the individual components of the framework (human-in-the-loop, prompt engineering, LLM-as-a-judge) have been explored in prior work, the *combination* and *specific adaptation* to resource-constrained scenarios are what distinguishes this work.

*   **Significance:** The paper has the potential to significantly impact the field of AI for social good, especially in healthcare. By providing a methodology that enables the co-design of LLM applications even when access to domain experts is limited, it can help democratize AI development and ensure that these technologies are tailored to the specific needs of underserved communities. The case study highlights the potential for LLMs to augment the capabilities of resource-strapped social workers and improve patient care.  The paper's emphasis on careful definition and validation of attributes addresses a crucial issue in ensuring the trustworthiness and reliability of LLM applications in high-stakes domains.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Real-world Application:** The paper is grounded in a practical, real-world problem, making the research highly relevant and impactful.
    *   **Detailed Methodology:** The multi-tier cascading framework is clearly described, providing a roadmap for other researchers and practitioners.
    *   **Evidence of Effectiveness:** The evaluation results, demonstrating improvements in concordance and agreement with human annotators, provide strong support for the effectiveness of the proposed approach.
    *   **Acknowledgement of limitations:** the study acknowledges limitations (2 attributes were added at the end of the study before evaluation) and suggests future steps to improve the application.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Generalizability:** While the case study focuses on a safety-net hospital, more discussion on how the framework might be adapted to other resource-constrained settings (e.g., different domains or community groups) would strengthen the paper.
    *   **LLM details:**  A slightly more thorough description of the model parameter/ settings would strengthen the scientific reproducibility of the work.
    *   **LLM-as-a-judge limitations:** Could address the limitations of using LLM as a judge, especially in capturing more nuanced aspects of context and subjectivity.

*   **Potential Influence:** The framework has the potential to influence the design of future LLM applications in resource-constrained settings, particularly in healthcare and social services. It also provides a valuable set of lessons learned for researchers and practitioners working in this area. The emphasis on carefully defining and validating attributes can help improve the trustworthiness and reliability of LLM applications in high-stakes domains.

**Rigorous Rationale for the Score:**

The paper presents a novel and significant contribution to the field by addressing a practical problem in a resource-constrained setting. The multi-tier cascading framework provides a structured and effective approach to co-designing LLM applications when access to domain experts is limited.  The evaluation results demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. However, the weaknesses related to generalizability and detail on the LLM performance slightly temper the overall impact.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### Steerable Pluralism: Pluralistic Alignment via Few-Shot Comparative Regression
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08509v1)
- **Authors**: Jadie Adams, Brian Hu, Emily Veenhuis, David Joy, Bharadwaj Ravichandran, Aaron Bray, Anthony Hoogs, Arslan Basharat
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are currently aligned using techniques such as reinforcement learning from human feedback (RLHF). However, these methods use scalar rewards that can only reflect user preferences on average. Pluralistic alignment instead seeks to capture diverse user preferences across a set of attributes, moving beyond just helpfulness and harmlessness. Toward this end, we propose a steerable pluralistic model based on few-shot comparative regression that can adapt to individual user preferences. Our approach leverages in-context learning and reasoning, grounded in a set of fine-grained attributes, to compare response options and make aligned choices. To evaluate our algorithm, we also propose two new steerable pluralistic benchmarks by adapting the Moral Integrity Corpus (MIC) and the HelpSteer2 datasets, demonstrating the applicability of our approach to value-aligned decision-making and reward modeling, respectively. Our few-shot comparative regression approach is interpretable and compatible with different attributes and LLMs, while outperforming multiple baseline and state-of-the-art methods. Our work provides new insights and research directions in pluralistic alignment, enabling a more fair and representative use of LLMs and advancing the state-of-the-art in ethical AI.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach called "Steerable Pluralism" to improve the alignment of Large Language Models (LLMs) with diverse human values.  It moves beyond single-scalar reward models (like RLHF) that only reflect average user preferences and instead aims to capture a spectrum of preferences across multiple attributes (like care, fairness, liberty, etc.).  The core of the approach is a "few-shot comparative regression" method, where the LLM is prompted to score multiple candidate responses with respect to these attributes, and the response best matching a specified target attribute profile is selected. The paper also addresses the lack of appropriate benchmarks by reframing two existing datasets, the Moral Integrity Corpus (MIC) and HelpSteer2, into steerable pluralistic benchmarks. Finally, the paper demonstrates the effectiveness of its approach compared to baselines and state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper offers several novel contributions. The few-shot comparative regression approach for steerable pluralistic alignment is a valuable addition to the toolbox of ethical AI techniques. The reframing of MIC and HelpSteer2 into steerable benchmarks is also significant, as it fills a critical gap in the field by providing resources for evaluating pluralistic alignment strategies. Moreover, the paper is the first to characterize implicit biases of instruction-tuned LLMs and reward models across attribute dimensions, providing valuable insights for future research.
*   **Significance:** The paper addresses a key challenge in ethical AI: aligning LLMs with diverse human values. By moving beyond single-scalar rewards, the paper makes a concrete step towards more nuanced and fair AI systems. The steerable pluralistic benchmarks and associated code will facilitate further research in this area. Furthermore, the paper provides a solution that is relatively generalizable, requiring no pretraining of the reward models and working across different LLMs.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The comparative regression approach is interpretable. The reasoning statements encourage chain-of-thought reasoning, which can facilitate understanding of model behavior.
    *   The approach is modular and can be applied to different LLMs.
    *   The reframing of existing datasets into steerable benchmarks is a practical and valuable contribution.
    *   The paper provides a strong experimental evaluation, comparing the proposed approach against several baselines and state-of-the-art methods.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Runtime:** The approach is computationally intensive. As the authors acknowledge, the proposed few-shot comparative regression approach takes longer to select a response than the comparison methods, which limits its practicality.
    *   **Prompt Engineering:** While the authors utilize in-context learning, the prompt engineering could be explored further. The prompts are complex and may be sensitive to slight variations. Although there is experimentation over the number of self-consistency samples, the prompt design could be a valuable avenue for additional work.
    *   **Limited LLM Backbone Size Ablation:** The backbone size ablation primarily compares a 3B and 70B parameter model, which shows performance improvements using the 70B model. However, a more granular performance exploration of intermediate sizes could potentially improve the validity of these results.
    *   The improvement is not always large, and the prompt-aligned baseline sometimes performs competitively.
    *   The benchmarks, while useful, are still limited in scope. MIC focuses on a specific type of moral reasoning, and HelpSteer2 centers around a specific set of preference attributes. Broadening the datasets with wider contexts could improve the applicability of the approach.

*   **Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field in several ways. It offers a practical approach for improving pluralistic alignment in LLMs, provides valuable benchmarks, and identifies biases in existing models. Future research could build on this work by exploring more efficient implementations of the comparative regression approach, developing more comprehensive steerable benchmarks, and investigating the transferability of learned attribute representations across different domains.

Score: 7

**Rationale:** The paper presents a solid and novel approach to a relevant problem, backed by a strong experimental evaluation. The provided benchmark is helpful.  However, the high computational cost, the need for further prompt optimization, and the limited scope of the benchmarks prevents it from scoring higher. Nevertheless, the contribution is significant and likely to spur further research in the field.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 7/10

### Using LLMs to Capture Users' Temporal Context for Recommendation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08512v1)
- **Authors**: Milad Sabouri, Masoud Mansoury, Kun Lin, Bamshad Mobasher
- **Abstract**: Effective recommender systems demand dynamic user understanding, especially in complex, evolving environments. Traditional user profiling often fails to capture the nuanced, temporal contextual factors of user preferences, such as transient short-term interests and enduring long-term tastes. This paper presents an assessment of Large Language Models (LLMs) for generating semantically rich, time-aware user profiles. We do not propose a novel end-to-end recommendation architecture; instead, the core contribution is a systematic investigation into the degree of LLM effectiveness in capturing the dynamics of user context by disentangling short-term and long-term preferences. This approach, framing temporal preferences as dynamic user contexts for recommendations, adaptively fuses these distinct contextual components into comprehensive user embeddings. The evaluation across Movies&TV and Video Games domains suggests that while LLM-generated profiles offer semantic depth and temporal structure, their effectiveness for context-aware recommendations is notably contingent on the richness of user interaction histories. Significant gains are observed in dense domains (e.g., Movies&TV), whereas improvements are less pronounced in sparse environments (e.g., Video Games). This work highlights LLMs' nuanced potential in enhancing user profiling for adaptive, context-aware recommendations, emphasizing the critical role of dataset characteristics for practical applicability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper explores using Large Language Models (LLMs) to capture users' temporal context for recommendations.  Instead of proposing a new end-to-end recommendation system, it focuses on assessing how well LLMs can distinguish between short-term and long-term user preferences. It generates separate natural language summaries for recent (short-term) and historical (long-term) interactions, converts these summaries into embeddings, and adaptively fuses them using an attention mechanism to create a comprehensive user embedding.  The evaluation, performed on Movies&TV and Video Games datasets, shows that LLM-generated profiles offer semantic depth and temporal structure, but their effectiveness depends on the richness of user interaction histories. Significant gains are observed in denser domains like Movies&TV, while improvements are less pronounced in sparse environments like Video Games.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its focused and systematic evaluation of LLMs for capturing temporal dynamics in user preferences, rather than presenting a complete, new recommendation architecture. The idea of using LLMs to generate user profiles has been explored, but the explicit separation and adaptive fusion of short-term and long-term preferences, combined with an analysis of how data density influences the LLMs' performance, is a valuable contribution. Prior LLM-based methods generate static summaries, neglecting the transient nature of user interests, a gap this paper addresses. However, the specific architectural components (attention-based fusion, use of BERT, etc.) are individually well-established techniques. The novel aspect is their targeted application and evaluation in this particular context.

*   **Significance:** The findings of the study are significant because they provide practical insights into the conditional effectiveness of leveraging LLMs for enhanced dynamic user representation in context-aware recommendation settings. The demonstration that the benefits of LLMs are more pronounced in denser interaction domains is crucial for real-world application, influencing where and how these computationally intensive models should be deployed. It highlights the importance of considering dataset characteristics when designing LLM-driven recommender systems, a factor often overlooked in other studies. Further, this work is a direct contribution to improving the context-aware recommender systems (CARS) body of literature by detailing the impact of LLMs on improving overall performance.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear research question and focused scope:** The paper has a well-defined objective (assessing LLMs' ability to capture temporal user dynamics).
    *   **Systematic evaluation:** The ablation study is well-designed and provides valuable insights into the contribution of each component (temporal separation, attention-based fusion).
    *   **Practical relevance:** The findings have direct implications for practitioners considering using LLMs in recommender systems. The distinction between dense and sparse environments is especially useful.
    *   **Reproducibility:** Code and prompts are publicly available, aiding reproducibility.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited baseline comparison:** While Centric, Temp-Fusion, Matrix Factorization, and Popularity are reasonable baselines, the paper could benefit from comparing against more sophisticated temporal recommendation models that do not rely on LLMs (e.g., traditional sequence models).
    *   **Reliance on SBERT:** The use of SBERT for embedding could be seen as a potential bottleneck. Exploring other embedding techniques or fine-tuning the LLM itself for embedding generation might yield further improvements.
    *   **Domain specificity:** The conclusions are drawn from two specific datasets (Movies&TV, Video Games). While these datasets are representative of certain interaction patterns, generalizability to other domains (e.g., e-commerce, news) needs further investigation.
    *   **GPT-4-mini**: While cost-effective, it still adds a computational burden to the system.

*   **Impact and Potential Influence:** The paper's focused scope and clear findings have the potential to influence future research in LLM-based recommender systems. It may lead to more nuanced approaches to user profiling, considering the temporal dimension and the characteristics of the interaction data. Further, it may encourage more comparative studies that isolate the contributions of LLMs relative to alternative techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel evaluation and analysis of LLMs for temporal user profiling in recommender systems. It identifies and examines a critical issue: how data density impacts the effectiveness of LLM-driven profiles. While some architectural elements leverage existing techniques, the systematic approach to separating short-term and long-term contexts and assessing the conditions under which LLMs are most beneficial makes a valuable contribution to the field. The weaknesses, such as the reliance on SBERT and the limited domain scope, are reasonable given the paper's focus, and they provide clear directions for future research. Therefore, I believe that this paper is a solid contribution that provides specific performance observations that are otherwise neglected.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.IR
- **Score**: 7/10

### SynLLM: A Comparative Analysis of Large Language Models for Medical Tabular Synthetic Data Generation via Prompt Engineering
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08529v1)
- **Authors**: Arshia Ilaty, Hossein Shirazi, Hajar Homayouni
- **Abstract**: Access to real-world medical data is often restricted due to privacy regulations, posing a significant barrier to the advancement of healthcare research. Synthetic data offers a promising alternative; however, generating realistic, clinically valid, and privacy-conscious records remains a major challenge. Recent advancements in Large Language Models (LLMs) offer new opportunities for structured data generation; however, existing approaches frequently lack systematic prompting strategies and comprehensive, multi-dimensional evaluation frameworks. In this paper, we present SynLLM, a modular framework for generating high-quality synthetic medical tabular data using 20 state-of-the-art open-source LLMs, including LLaMA, Mistral, and GPT variants, guided by structured prompts. We propose four distinct prompt types, ranging from example-driven to rule-based constraints, that encode schema, metadata, and domain knowledge to control generation without model fine-tuning. Our framework features a comprehensive evaluation pipeline that rigorously assesses generated data across statistical fidelity, clinical consistency, and privacy preservation. We evaluate SynLLM across three public medical datasets, including Diabetes, Cirrhosis, and Stroke, using 20 open-source LLMs. Our results show that prompt engineering significantly impacts data quality and privacy risk, with rule-based prompts achieving the best privacy-quality balance. SynLLM establishes that, when guided by well-designed prompts and evaluated with robust, multi-metric criteria, LLMs can generate synthetic medical data that is both clinically plausible and privacy-aware, paving the way for safer and more effective data sharing in healthcare research.
- **Summary**: Okay, I can provide a summary and a critical evaluation of the provided paper.

**Summary:**

The paper "SynLLM: A Comparative Analysis of Large Language Models for Medical Tabular Synthetic Data Generation via Prompt Engineering" introduces SynLLM, a modular framework that uses structured prompts to guide Large Language Models (LLMs) in generating high-quality synthetic medical tabular data. The authors evaluate 20 open-source LLMs (including LLaMA, Mistral, and GPT variants) across three public medical datasets (Diabetes, Cirrhosis, and Stroke).  They propose four distinct prompt types, ranging from example-driven to rule-based, to control the generation process without model fine-tuning. The paper assesses the generated data based on statistical fidelity, clinical consistency, and privacy preservation, demonstrating that prompt engineering significantly impacts data quality and privacy risks, with rule-based prompts achieving the best balance between privacy and quality.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's novelty lies primarily in its systematic exploration of prompt engineering strategies for generating medical tabular data using LLMs, coupled with a comprehensive evaluation framework. While the use of LLMs for synthetic data generation isn't entirely new, SynLLM's structured prompt taxonomy (SEEDEX, FEATDESC, STATGUIDE, CLINRULE) and the multi-dimensional evaluation pipeline are a distinct contribution. Also, the work's focus on medical data with privacy considerations adds to its relevance.

**Significance:** The paper addresses a critical challenge in healthcare AI: the limited access to real-world medical data due to privacy regulations. By demonstrating that carefully crafted prompts can enable LLMs to generate realistic and privacy-aware synthetic data, the research has the potential to facilitate safer and more effective data sharing for research and model development. The findings about the effectiveness of rule-based prompts (CLINRULE) are particularly significant, as they provide a pathway towards prioritizing privacy without sacrificing data utility.

**Strengths:**

*   **Systematic Approach:** The authors' structured approach to prompt engineering, with a clear taxonomy of prompt types, allows for a controlled evaluation of different prompting strategies.
*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The multi-dimensional evaluation framework, encompassing statistical fidelity, clinical consistency, and privacy, provides a holistic assessment of the generated data.
*   **Practical Relevance:** The focus on medical data and privacy concerns makes the research highly relevant to real-world applications in healthcare AI.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and clearly presents the methodology, results, and analysis.
*   **Emphasis on Privacy:** The focus on prompt engineering for privacy preservation is a timely and important contribution.
*   **Large-Scale Analysis**: The analysis with 20 models is valuable in identifying models that are well suited to this type of task, and also to find commonalities and differences across models.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Privacy Guarantees:** The paper relies on empirical privacy metrics (k-anonymity, nearest-neighbor distance) rather than formal privacy guarantees like differential privacy. While these metrics provide insights into disclosure risk, they don't offer the same level of protection.
*   **Limited Diversity of Datasets:** While the datasets selected are representative of tabular medical data, expanding the evaluation to include a more diverse set of medical datasets could strengthen the generalizability of the findings.
*   **Limited model selection**: The paper is limited to open-source models. The best open-source models for a task like this one may not be as good as closed-source counterparts such as GPT-4.
*   **Heuristic Privacy**: The paper relies on heuristic privacy instead of formal privacy methods. It would be beneficial to investigate incorporating Differential Privacy (DP) into the framework, for example via differentially-private fine tuning (although this wasn't the main contribution of the work).

**Potential Influence:**

The paper has the potential to influence the field of synthetic data generation for healthcare AI by:

*   Providing a practical framework for generating realistic and privacy-aware medical data using LLMs.
*   Highlighting the importance of prompt engineering in controlling the quality and privacy of synthetic data.
*   Guiding researchers and practitioners in selecting appropriate LLMs and prompting strategies for their specific needs.
*   Inspiring further research into more robust privacy guarantees for synthetic medical data.

**Justification for Score:**

Given the strengths and weaknesses outlined above, I assign the paper a score of **7**. The paper presents a valuable and systematic exploration of a relevant problem, but it has some limitations. Although the empirical privacy evaluations are relevant, this isn't enough to assign a score of 8. With these limitations, and also because the paper hasn't been adopted in the field yet (early work), it is hard to justify a higher score.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.AI
- **Score**: 7/10

### Profiling Large Language Model Inference on Apple Silicon: A Quantization Perspective
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08531v1)
- **Authors**: Afsara Benazir, Felix Xiaozhu Lin
- **Abstract**: A systematic understanding of Apple Silicon is lacking in the current landscape of hardware efficiency; research focus is largely centered on accelerating GPUs for large-scale training or inference on CUDA devices. This paper investigates Apple Silicon's unique memory architecture that offers a unified memory integrating CPU and GPU memory and its implications for on-device LLM inference. We decipher myths about whether Apple Silicon is efficient for on-device inference compared to competitors such as NVIDIA GPUs by directly conducting latency and throughput comparison benchmarks. We explain the performance gap between them through profiling low level hardware metrics - ALU utilization, memory bandwidth, buffer usage, cache residency etc. at runtime. We draw several insights regarding performance bottlenecks such as dequantization overhead, compute throughput and memory bandwidth. We debunk existing false claims regarding large language model inference such as compressing models to lower bit precision is a defacto promise for faster inference across all hardware platforms. We find that the large unified memory enables Apple Silicon to be both cost effective and efficient against NVIDIA GPUs for ultra large language models. Our large scale evaluation on 5 hardware testbeds incorporating three Apple M-series devices: M2 Ultra, M2 Max and M4 Pro and two NVIDIA GPUs: NVIDIA RTX A6000, a multi GPU setup with 2xNVIDIA RTX A6000, 5 model scales ranging from 8B to 405B parameters and 14 quantization schemes gives an understanding of how Apple Silicon fits within the paradigm of on-device LLM inference. Our analysis reveals multiple resource interdependencies and unexpected findings, while also quantifying established insights. To the best of our knowledge, this study makes the first attempt to present a thorough characterization and analysis of Apple Silicon for on-device inference.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper presents a comprehensive analysis of large language model (LLM) inference on Apple Silicon, focusing on the impact of quantization techniques. It compares Apple Silicon's performance against NVIDIA GPUs, considering both latency and cost-effectiveness.  Through extensive benchmarking and profiling, the authors identify performance bottlenecks such as dequantization overhead, memory bandwidth limitations, and the lack of dedicated compute units similar to NVIDIA's tensor cores. The paper challenges the assumption that lower bit precision always leads to faster inference and reveals that Apple Silicon's unified memory architecture enables efficient inference of ultra-large models, making it a cost-effective alternative to NVIDIA GPUs in certain scenarios. The study analyzes various quantization schemes, concluding that block-based quantization is superior to codebook-based quantization on Apple Silicon due to hardware limitations.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The paper's primary strength lies in being one of the first comprehensive analyses of LLM inference specifically on Apple Silicon. While some prior works have touched on Apple GPU performance or quantization, this study uniquely combines a broad range of model sizes, quantization schemes, and detailed hardware profiling. The debunking of common misconceptions about low-bit quantization and its nuanced impact across different hardware architectures is also valuable.

**Significance:** The work has practical significance for ML practitioners seeking to deploy LLMs on edge devices or in cost-sensitive environments. The findings provide actionable recommendations for model selection and quantization strategies tailored to Apple Silicon.  The insights into hardware bottlenecks can also inform future hardware design and optimization for LLM inference. Highlighting the cost-effectiveness of Apple Silicon for ultra-large models is particularly relevant, as it widens the accessibility of LLM technology.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The paper utilizes a wide array of models, quantization schemes, and hardware platforms, providing a robust and well-supported analysis.
*   **Detailed Profiling:** The use of Apple Instruments and other profiling tools allows for a fine-grained understanding of hardware performance counters and bottlenecks.
*   **Actionable Recommendations:** The paper concludes with clear recommendations for practitioners and hardware vendors.
*   **Clear Presentation:** The paper is well-written and organized, making the findings accessible to a broad audience.
*   **Addresses an Important Gap:** The research fills a gap in the existing literature by focusing on Apple Silicon, which has been relatively understudied in the context of LLM inference.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Limited Hardware Scope:** While the comparison with NVIDIA GPUs is valuable, including a broader range of NVIDIA architectures or other hardware accelerators (e.g., AMD GPUs, TPUs) would strengthen the analysis.
*   **Software Library Choice:** The choice of llama.cpp for inference introduces potential biases tied to the specific optimizations and limitations of that library. Exploring inference engines with different underlying implementations could paint a more complete picture.
*   **Benchmarking Details:**  Details of the benchmarking could be better defined (e.g., the prompts being used for inference).

**Justification for Score:**

The paper makes a significant contribution to the field by providing a systematic and data-driven analysis of LLM inference on Apple Silicon. It challenges existing assumptions and offers practical guidance for practitioners. The detailed profiling and analysis of hardware bottlenecks are particularly valuable. However, the limited hardware scope and software library used slightly restrict the generalizability of the findings. This work opens avenues for future research directions focusing on novel quantization techniques tailored for Apple Silicon. While valuable and timely, this analysis is largely descriptive rather than generating a revolutionary model.

**Score: 7.5**

### OmniLLP: Enhancing LLM-based Log Level Prediction with Context-Aware Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08545v1)
- **Authors**: Youssef Esseddiq Ouatiti, Mohammed Sayagh, Bram Adams, Ahmed E. Hassan
- **Abstract**: Developers insert logging statements in source code to capture relevant runtime information essential for maintenance and debugging activities. Log level choice is an integral, yet tricky part of the logging activity as it controls log verbosity and therefore influences systems' observability and performance. Recent advances in ML-based log level prediction have leveraged large language models (LLMs) to propose log level predictors (LLPs) that demonstrated promising performance improvements (AUC between 0.64 and 0.8). Nevertheless, current LLM-based LLPs rely on randomly selected in-context examples, overlooking the structure and the diverse logging practices within modern software projects. In this paper, we propose OmniLLP, a novel LLP enhancement framework that clusters source files based on (1) semantic similarity reflecting the code's functional purpose, and (2) developer ownership cohesion. By retrieving in-context learning examples exclusively from these semantic and ownership aware clusters, we aim to provide more coherent prompts to LLPs leveraging LLMs, thereby improving their predictive accuracy. Our results show that both semantic and ownership-aware clusterings statistically significantly improve the accuracy (by up to 8\% AUC) of the evaluated LLM-based LLPs compared to random predictors (i.e., leveraging randomly selected in-context examples from the whole project). Additionally, our approach that combines the semantic and ownership signal for in-context prediction achieves an impressive 0.88 to 0.96 AUC across our evaluated projects. Our findings highlight the value of integrating software engineering-specific context, such as code semantic and developer ownership signals into LLM-LLPs, offering developers a more accurate, contextually-aware approach to logging and therefore, enhancing system maintainability and observability.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "OmniLLP: Enhancing LLM-based Log Level Prediction with Context-Aware Retrieval":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces OmniLLP, a framework that enhances log level prediction (LLP) by leveraging large language models (LLMs) with a novel context-aware retrieval mechanism.  OmniLLP addresses the limitation of existing LLM-based LLPs that treat software projects as monolithic entities, ignoring the influence of code semantics and developer ownership on logging practices.  It achieves this by clustering source files based on semantic similarity (code functionality) and developer ownership, then retrieving in-context learning (ICL) examples from these clusters to provide more coherent prompts to the LLMs. The paper shows that this approach improves predictive accuracy (measured by AUC) compared to random retrieval of ICL examples. They also demonstrate that a multiplex clustering approach that combines both semantic intent and developer ownership yields the best results.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in its integration of software engineering-specific context into LLM-based LLPs. While prior works have explored LLMs for LLP and local models for other software engineering tasks, the combination of semantic clustering and developer ownership clustering to inform ICL retrieval for log level prediction is new.  The multiplex clustering approach that combines semantic and ownership signals is also a novel aspect.  The paper moves beyond treating projects as uniform codebases, which is a simplification present in many existing LLM applications in SE.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practically important problem – automating log level selection – and proposes a solution that demonstrably improves upon existing approaches. By providing more accurate and contextually aware log level predictions, OmniLLP has the potential to reduce software maintainability costs by making systems more observable, streamlining debugging, and minimizing performance overhead. The findings are valuable because they highlight the importance of contextualizing LLM applications with SE-specific knowledge. While the improvement may appear incremental in some instances, even small increases in AUC for a task like log level prediction can lead to substantial benefits. The exploration of the relationship between code's functional intent, developer collaboration, and appropriate log-level choices contributes to a deeper understanding of logging practices in modern software systems.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Empirical Rigor:** The paper presents a well-designed empirical evaluation on four large, widely used open-source Java projects. They use appropriate metrics and statistical tests, and compare OmniLLP against reasonable baselines.
    * **Clear Motivation and Problem Definition:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of log level selection and the limitations of existing LLM-based LLPs.
    * **Well-Defined Approach:**  The methodology is well-defined and replicable.  The paper provides sufficient details on the clustering techniques and the experimental setup.
    * **Analysis and Discussion:** The paper provides insightful analysis of the results, including the factors that influence the performance of OmniLLP and the limitations of the approach.
    * **Near-Real-Time Retrieval Speed:** The latency analysis of retrieval step, showcasing the retrieval mechanism is efficient and can operate in near-real-time is highly beneficial.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Language and Project Specificity:** The evaluation is limited to Java projects.  It is not clear how well the approach would generalize to other programming languages or software ecosystems.
    * **Reliance on CodeXEmbed:** The paper relies on the CodeXEmbed model for semantic encoding. The performance of OmniLLP may be sensitive to the choice of the embedding model, and future work could explore other options.
    * **Noise Files:** A high portion of files marked as 'noise' after clustering. This means that the retrieval reverts to baseline retrieval. Addressing this in future work could further improve the approach.
    * **Incremental Improvements:** While the AUC improvements are statistically significant, some of them are relatively small (e.g., 2-4%). The practical impact of these small improvements might be questioned by some. However, even a small increase in AUC can lead to meaningful improvements in debugging and maintenance.
    * **Limited Qualitative Assessment:** While there's a performance improvement,  a more detailed qualitative analysis that shows specific instances of where the approach excels compared to prior approaches could enhance the paper's practical value.

* **Potential Influence:**  The paper has the potential to influence the development of more context-aware LLM-based software engineering tools. It demonstrates the value of integrating software engineering-specific knowledge, such as code semantics and developer ownership, into LLM applications.  The ideas presented in the paper could also inspire new approaches to clustering and retrieval in other software engineering tasks.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach to enhance log level prediction by leveraging LLMs and context-aware retrieval.  The results are promising, and the paper addresses a practically important problem. While the improvements over baselines are sometimes modest, they are statistically significant and show the potential of the proposed approach.  The limitations are clearly acknowledged. Taking all of this into account, and emphasizing a balanced view between novelty and scope,
