### Calibration Attention: Instance-wise Temperature Scaling for Vision Transformers
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08547v1)
- **Authors**: Wenhao Liang, Wei Emma Zhang, Lin Yue, Miao Xu, Olaf Maennel, Weitong Chen
- **Abstract**: Probability calibration is critical when Vision Transformers are deployed in risk-sensitive applications. The standard fix, post-hoc temperature scaling, uses a single global scalar and requires a held-out validation set. We introduce Calibration Attention (CalAttn), a drop-in module that learns an adaptive, per-instance temperature directly from the ViT's CLS token. Across CIFAR-10/100, MNIST, Tiny-ImageNet, and ImageNet-1K, CalAttn reduces calibration error by up to 4x on ViT-224, DeiT, and Swin, while adding under 0.1 percent additional parameters. The learned temperatures cluster tightly around 1.0, in contrast to the large global values used by standard temperature scaling. CalAttn is simple, efficient, and architecture-agnostic, and yields more trustworthy probabilities without sacrificing accuracy. Code: [https://github.com/EagleAdelaide/CalibrationAttention-CalAttn-](https://github.com/EagleAdelaide/CalibrationAttention-CalAttn-)
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces Calibration Attention (CalAttn), a novel drop-in module for Vision Transformers (ViTs) designed to improve probability calibration.  Unlike traditional temperature scaling which uses a single global scalar, CalAttn learns a dynamic, per-instance temperature directly from the ViT's CLS token embedding using a small two-layer MLP.  This allows the model to adapt confidence based on sample-specific uncertainty.  Experiments on CIFAR, MNIST, TinyImageNet and ImageNet demonstrate substantial reductions in calibration error compared to vanilla ViTs and comparable CNNs, with minimal overhead in parameters. The paper also provides analysis showing the CLS token norm correlates with sample difficulty, justifying the design choice.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:**  The core idea of learning per-instance temperatures for calibration is a notable advancement.  While the concept of adaptive temperature scaling isn't entirely new, the way this paper achieves it *within* the ViT architecture, leveraging the CLS token, is novel. The connection between the CLS token norm and sample difficulty is also a valuable contribution, providing a concrete cue for adaptive calibration.  However, the CalAttn module itself, a two-layer MLP, is relatively simple. The paper is the *first* VIT-compatible module and makes a contribution over VIT-Calib.

**Significance:**

*   **Improved Calibration:** The demonstrated reduction in calibration error is significant, particularly given the safety-critical applications (e.g., medical diagnosis, autonomous driving) where reliable probabilities are essential. The fact that it outperforms common calibration methods like temperature scaling on both CNNs and ViTs highlights its practical relevance. The paper clearly shows that previous results showing VITs to be 'naturally' better-calibrated are insufficient and that proper tuning is needed.
*   **Minimal Overhead:**  The small parameter count (less than 0.1%) makes CalAttn easily deployable and applicable to various ViT architectures. It shows that improved calibration doesn’t have to come at the cost of significantly increased model complexity.
*   **End-to-End Training:** The integration of calibration directly into the training objective is a key strength, allowing the backbone to learn calibration-aware representations. This avoids the decoupling issues of post-hoc temperature scaling.
*   **Limitations & Scope:** The architecture is explicitly designed for ViTs and relies on the existence of a global CLS token or GAP feature. The extent to which the module can be directly applied or adapted to CNNs or other architectures without such a global token is not explored in detail.
*   **Further Improvements:** Despite the significant gains, the paper could explore the method's robustness and performance in scenarios beyond common datasets. This opens the door to future improvements. Further exploration is done to see the performance and robustness of OoD data shift in the Appendix.
*   **Potential Impact:** The simple design and ease of integration suggest that CalAttn could become a standard component in ViT-based systems, especially those where trustworthy confidence estimates are important. It could also spur further research into more sophisticated, architecture-aware calibration techniques.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a novel and practical method for improving calibration in ViTs. The approach leverages intrinsic properties of the ViT architecture to achieve state-of-the-art results with minimal overhead. While the core module (MLP) itself is simple, its integration, the justification for its input, and the demonstration of significant calibration improvements warrant a high score. The limitations of being targeted at ViTs and its need of a class/global token do limit the score, however.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Unlocking the Potential of Diffusion Priors in Blind Face Restoration
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08556v1)
- **Authors**: Yunqi Miao, Zhiyu Qu, Mingqi Gao, Changrui Chen, Jifei Song, Jungong Han, Jiankang Deng
- **Abstract**: Although diffusion prior is rising as a powerful solution for blind face restoration (BFR), the inherent gap between the vanilla diffusion model and BFR settings hinders its seamless adaptation. The gap mainly stems from the discrepancy between 1) high-quality (HQ) and low-quality (LQ) images and 2) synthesized and real-world images. The vanilla diffusion model is trained on images with no or less degradations, whereas BFR handles moderately to severely degraded images. Additionally, LQ images used for training are synthesized by a naive degradation model with limited degradation patterns, which fails to simulate complex and unknown degradations in real-world scenarios. In this work, we use a unified network FLIPNET that switches between two modes to resolve specific gaps. In Restoration mode, the model gradually integrates BFR-oriented features and face embeddings from LQ images to achieve authentic and faithful face restoration. In Degradation mode, the model synthesizes real-world like degraded images based on the knowledge learned from real-world degradation datasets. Extensive evaluations on benchmark datasets show that our model 1) outperforms previous diffusion prior based BFR methods in terms of authenticity and fidelity, and 2) outperforms the naive degradation model in modeling the real-world degradations.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper on blind face restoration (BFR):

**Summary:**

The paper addresses limitations of existing diffusion model (DM)-based BFR methods. These limitations stem from the discrepancy between the vanilla diffusion prior trained on mostly pristine images and the BFR task that deals with significantly degraded faces.  Additionally, the synthetic degradation models used to create training data often fail to capture the complexities of real-world degradations.  The authors propose a unified network, FLIPNET, that leverages a large-scale text-to-image (T2I) model.  FLIPNET operates in two modes: 1) a restoration mode that integrates BFR-oriented features and face embeddings from low-quality (LQ) images to restore authentic faces, and 2) a degradation mode that learns from real-world degraded images to synthesize realistic degraded images.  By flipping between these modes, FLIPNET aims to overcome the limitations of previous methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the unified architecture (FLIPNET) and the specific strategy of flipping between restoration and degradation modes. While using DMs and T2I models for BFR isn't entirely new, the approach of explicitly learning and synthesizing real-world degradations *within the same network* as the restoration task is a key differentiator.  The BoostHub for selectively integrating LQ features and the exploration of BFR-oriented face embeddings also add to the novelty, though their individual contributions might be incremental.

*   **Significance:** The paper tackles a crucial issue in BFR: the unrealistic nature of synthetic degradations and the mismatch between training and real-world data.  If FLIPNET can demonstrably generate more realistic training data and improve restoration performance on real-world images, it would be a significant contribution. The claimed improvements in authenticity and fidelity, if robustly validated, are also highly desirable.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Definition:** Clearly identifies the limitations of existing DM-based BFR methods.
    *   **Unified Approach:**  Addresses both the LQ/HQ image gap and the synthetic/real-world degradation gap with a single model.
    *   **Modularity:** The BoostHub provides a controlled way to integrate LQ features, balancing fidelity and authenticity.
    *   **Experimental Validation:** Claims to outperform previous methods on benchmark datasets (although more details from the experiments would solidify the evaluation).

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Reliance on T2I Model:** The core of FLIPNET relies on a pretrained T2I model.  While leveraging these models is a good strategy, the results may be limited by the base model's capabilities. The paper should more explicitly state how much the base model's architecture affects performance.
    *   **Complexity:**  The two-mode operation and the integration of multiple components (BoostHub, face embeddings, LoRA fine-tuning) add complexity to the model. A simpler ablation study of the relative contributions of each component would be helpful.
    *   **"Real-world" Degradation Data:** The paper mentions using diverse datasets to capture degradation information.  A more detailed description of these datasets, how they were selected and processed, and what specific degradations each dataset contributes would strengthen the paper.
    *   **Generalizability:**  Although experiments are conducted, the paper does not explore the generalization ability of the model under conditions such as different ethnicities.

* **Justification for Score:** Considering the novelty, significance, strengths, and weaknesses, I assign a score of **7**. The idea of a unified network that simultaneously learns to restore faces and synthesize realistic degradations is promising. The proposed FLIPNET architecture with BoostHub for feature integration shows technical merit. However, the reliance on a pre-trained T2I model and the complexity of the system warrant a lower score. Solid experimental results are presented, but a stronger ablative analysis of each component could strengthen the impact of the paper.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DocThinker: Explainable Multimodal Large Language Models with Rule-based Reinforcement Learning for Document Understanding
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08589v1)
- **Authors**: Wenwen Yu, Zhibo Yang, Yuliang Liu, Xiang Bai
- **Abstract**: Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs) have demonstrated remarkable capabilities in document understanding. However, their reasoning processes remain largely black-box, making it difficult to ensure reliability and trustworthiness, especially in high-stakes domains such as legal, financial, and medical document analysis. Existing methods use fixed Chain-of-Thought (CoT) reasoning with supervised fine-tuning (SFT) but suffer from catastrophic forgetting, poor adaptability, and limited generalization across domain tasks. In this paper, we propose DocThinker, a rule-based Reinforcement Learning (RL) framework for dynamic inference-time reasoning. Instead of relying on static CoT templates, DocThinker autonomously refines reasoning strategies via policy learning, generating explainable intermediate results, including structured reasoning processes, rephrased questions, regions of interest (RoI) supporting the answer, and the final answer. By integrating multi-objective rule-based rewards and KL-constrained optimization, our method mitigates catastrophic forgetting and enhances both adaptability and transparency. Extensive experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that DocThinker significantly improves generalization while producing more explainable and human-understandable reasoning steps. Our findings highlight RL as a powerful alternative for enhancing explainability and adaptability in MLLM-based document understanding. Code will be available at https://github.com/wenwenyu/DocThinker.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "DocThinker: Explainable Multimodal Large Language Models with Rule-based Reinforcement Learning for Document Understanding" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DocThinker, a novel framework for document understanding that leverages rule-based Reinforcement Learning (RL) to improve the explainability and adaptability of Multimodal Large Language Models (MLLMs).  Instead of relying on fixed Chain-of-Thought (CoT) templates or supervised fine-tuning (SFT), DocThinker uses RL to dynamically refine reasoning strategies.  It generates explainable intermediate results, including structured reasoning processes, rephrased questions, and Regions of Interest (RoI) supporting the answer. A key aspect is the integration of multi-objective rule-based rewards and KL-constrained optimization to mitigate catastrophic forgetting and enhance adaptability and transparency. Experiments on multiple benchmarks demonstrate that DocThinker significantly improves generalization while producing more human-understandable reasoning steps.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a combination of several interesting ideas, but its overall novelty is arguably incremental.

    *   **RL for Document Understanding:**  Using RL for document understanding isn't entirely new; some previous works explore RL for document-related tasks. However, *DocThinker's specific application of RL with rule-based rewards to enhance both explainability and adaptability in document MLLMs is a relatively novel contribution*.
    *   **Multi-Objective Rule-Based Rewards:**  The design of multi-objective reward functions for explainability is a valuable contribution. Defining clear rewards for XML output format, answer accuracy, RoI consistency, and rephrased question quality is a well-structured approach to guide the RL agent. While each individual reward component might not be entirely novel, their *combination and the impact on MLLM document understanding are novel*.
    *   **GRPO for Multimodal Reasoning:** Extending the Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) algorithm, previously applied in text-based tasks, to multimodal document understanding is a reasonable step. *However, it's an adaptation rather than a radical departure*.
    *   **Explainability:** The goal of enhanced explainability is valuable, but the specific techniques (rephrased questions, bounding boxes) are somewhat limited.

*   **Significance:**

    *   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more trustworthy and reliable document analysis systems, particularly in high-stakes domains.
    *   **Practicality:** The framework's emphasis on rule-based rewards makes it easier to implement and verify compared to relying on learned reward models.
    *   **Generalization:**  The demonstrated improvements in generalization compared to SFT-based methods are significant, addressing a key limitation of current MLLMs.
    *   **Clarity:** The writing is generally clear and well-organized, making the paper accessible to researchers in the field.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   The paper clearly articulates the problem of limited explainability in document MLLMs.
    *   The proposed DocThinker framework provides a structured and verifiable approach to improve both accuracy and explainability.
    *   The experimental results demonstrate a significant improvement in generalization and competitive performance on standard benchmarks.
    *   Ablation studies provide insights into the contribution of each reward component and the KL divergence regularization.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   The explainability techniques are somewhat basic. More sophisticated explainability methods (e.g., attention visualization, counterfactual analysis) could further enhance the framework.
    *   The computational cost of RL training can be substantial. The paper could benefit from a more detailed analysis of the training resources required and the efficiency of the approach.
    *   While the results showcase better zero-shot performance, the datasets used for these zero-shot benchmarks (DUDE, SROIE, V7W) might be more narrow than other benchmarks.
    *   The reliance on GPT-4 in hallucination evaluation might introduce biases, particularly in defining "correctness" or "semantic similarity".

*   **Reasoning for the Score:**
    The paper makes a solid contribution, improving the explainability and adaptability of MLLMs for document understanding through an RL-based framework. The combination of rule-based rewards, GRPO, and KL-constrained optimization provides a practical and effective approach. The experimental results support the claims of improved generalization. However, the novelty is incremental, and the explainability techniques are somewhat limited. The reliance on specific evaluation metrics and smaller zero-shot datasets introduces additional concerns.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.CV
- **Score**: 7/10

### DepressLLM: Interpretable domain-adapted language model for depression detection from real-world narratives
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08591v1)
- **Authors**: Sehwan Moon, Aram Lee, Jeong Eun Kim, Hee-Ju Kang, Il-Seon Shin, Sung-Wan Kim, Jae-Min Kim, Min Jhon, Ju-Wan Kim
- **Abstract**: Advances in large language models (LLMs) have enabled a wide range of applications. However, depression prediction is hindered by the lack of large-scale, high-quality, and rigorously annotated datasets. This study introduces DepressLLM, trained and evaluated on a novel corpus of 3,699 autobiographical narratives reflecting both happiness and distress. DepressLLM provides interpretable depression predictions and, via its Score-guided Token Probability Summation (SToPS) module, delivers both improved classification performance and reliable confidence estimates, achieving an AUC of 0.789, which rises to 0.904 on samples with confidence $\geq$ 0.95. To validate its robustness to heterogeneous data, we evaluated DepressLLM on in-house datasets, including an Ecological Momentary Assessment (EMA) corpus of daily stress and mood recordings, and on public clinical interview data. Finally, a psychiatric review of high-confidence misclassifications highlighted key model and data limitations that suggest directions for future refinements. These findings demonstrate that interpretable AI can enable earlier diagnosis of depression and underscore the promise of medical AI in psychiatry.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the DepressLLM paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces DepressLLM, an interpretable domain-adapted language model specifically designed for depression detection using real-world narrative data.  The model is trained and evaluated on a novel corpus called TREND-P, consisting of autobiographical narratives reflecting both happy and distressing memories.  DepressLLM uses a "Score-guided Token Probability Summation (STOPS)" module to improve classification performance and generate reliable confidence estimates. The authors demonstrate DepressLLM's performance on in-house datasets (including Ecological Momentary Assessment (EMA) data) and a public clinical interview dataset (DAIC-WOZ). Finally, they conduct a psychiatric review of misclassifications to identify model and data limitations.  The authors emphasize the potential for interpretable AI to enable earlier depression diagnosis and the promise of medical AI.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents several novel aspects. The creation and use of the TREND-P dataset, which is specifically designed to capture paired positive and negative contexts within individual narratives, is a significant contribution. The STOPS module, which enhances both performance and confidence estimation, adds a unique element to the architecture. Training on continuous PHQ-9 scores, rather than binary labels is a clever innovation.

*   **Significance:** Depression is a widespread mental health challenge, making automated detection tools valuable. The focus on interpretability is crucial in a medical domain where trust and understanding are paramount. While prior work has explored LLMs for mental health, DepressLLM distinguishes itself through its curated dataset, interpretable design, and evaluation across diverse data types. The psychiatric validation of errors provides actionable insights for future model refinement.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Dataset:** The TREND-P dataset addresses the scarcity of high-quality, rigorously annotated datasets for depression detection. The focus on both happy and distressing memories makes the data more nuanced.
    *   **Interpretability:** The STOPS module and the natural language explanations are excellent for building trust and understanding model decisions.
    *   **Comprehensive Evaluation:** The evaluation includes in-domain and out-of-domain datasets, providing a thorough assessment of robustness.
    *   **Error Analysis:** The psychiatric review adds significant value by identifying systematic errors and providing context-specific reasons for discrepancies between model predictions and ground truth.
    *   **Open-Source Approach:** Releasing the LoRA weights (which is essentially the core IP here) is great for open science, and allows others to build on top of the work.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Reliance on Retrospective Data:** The TREND-P dataset relies on participants' recollections of past events, which can be subject to recall bias and temporal insensitivity.
    *   **Self-Reported Labels:**  PHQ-9 scores, while standardized, are still self-reported and may not always accurately reflect clinical reality. The paper acknowledges this limitation.
    *   **Limited Generalizability Insights:** While the paper explores external datasets, a more detailed analysis of the types of data where DepressLLM generalizes most effectively would be useful. What data sources *shouldn't* it be used on?
    *   **STOPS Drop:** The drop in performance from adding STOPS seems concerning, and likely means the prompting approach needs re-engineering.

*   **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more reliable and trustworthy AI-based mental health screening tools. The dataset and the interpretable architecture can serve as a valuable resource for other researchers in the field. The focus on robust evaluation and error analysis sets a good example for responsible AI development in healthcare.

*   **Overall Assessment:** While the reliance on retrospective self-reported data presents a limitation, the paper addresses this with a thorough error analysis. The strengths related to the carefully constructed dataset, innovative STOPS method, comprehensive evaluation, and focus on interpretability outweigh the weaknesses.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper presents a significant contribution to the field by introducing a novel dataset and an interpretable model for depression detection. The strengths in dataset creation, model architecture, and thorough evaluation, as well as thoughtful error analysis, indicate a high-quality study with the potential to advance the development of more trustworthy and clinically relevant AI-based mental health tools.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### Yan: Foundational Interactive Video Generation
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08601v1)
- **Authors**: Yan Team
- **Abstract**: We present Yan, a foundational framework for interactive video generation, covering the entire pipeline from simulation and generation to editing. Specifically, Yan comprises three core modules. AAA-level Simulation: We design a highly-compressed, low-latency 3D-VAE coupled with a KV-cache-based shift-window denoising inference process, achieving real-time 1080P/60FPS interactive simulation. Multi-Modal Generation: We introduce a hierarchical autoregressive caption method that injects game-specific knowledge into open-domain multi-modal video diffusion models (VDMs), then transforming the VDM into a frame-wise, action-controllable, real-time infinite interactive video generator. Notably, when the textual and visual prompts are sourced from different domains, the model demonstrates strong generalization, allowing it to blend and compose the style and mechanics across domains flexibly according to user prompts. Multi-Granularity Editing: We propose a hybrid model that explicitly disentangles interactive mechanics simulation from visual rendering, enabling multi-granularity video content editing during interaction through text. Collectively, Yan offers an integration of these modules, pushing interactive video generation beyond isolated capabilities toward a comprehensive AI-driven interactive creation paradigm, paving the way for the next generation of creative tools, media, and entertainment. The project page is: https://greatx3.github.io/Yan/.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the "Yan: Foundational Interactive Video Generation" paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces "Yan," a comprehensive framework for interactive video generation (IGV).  Yan aims to address key limitations in existing IGV systems across three core modules: 1) AAA-level Simulation: Achieves real-time, high-fidelity (1080P/60FPS) interactive simulations via a compressed 3D-VAE and KV-cache-based denoising. 2) Multi-Modal Generation: Creates prompt-controllable and generalizable videos by injecting game-specific knowledge into diffusion models and transforming them for frame-wise control. 3) Multi-Granularity Editing:  Enables dynamic, multi-faceted editing (structure and style) during interaction by disentangling mechanics simulation from visual rendering. The paper details the architecture, training, and inference methods of each module and presents qualitative results demonstrating Yan's capabilities in diverse interactive scenarios.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper presents a strong combination of several novel elements.
    *   **AAA-Level Simulation:** The most compelling aspect is achieving 1080P/60FPS performance for a complex 3D game environment within an interactive setting. This is a significant advancement over existing game simulation techniques (detailed in the related work) that typically operate at lower resolutions and frame rates. The VAE compression and KV-cache inference are well-motivated and effective.
    *   **Multi-Modal Generation:** The hierarchical captioning approach is innovative and addresses the "anti-drifting" problem effectively in long video generation. Injecting game-specific knowledge into open-domain diffusion models is a practical way to improve generalization. The transformation into a frame-wise, action-controllable generator is also beneficial.
    *   **Multi-Granularity Editing:**  Disentangling mechanics simulation from visual rendering to enable multi-granularity editing during interaction is a creative approach.
*   **Significance:**  The significance of Yan is in its comprehensive approach to IGV. It moves beyond isolated functionalities to offer a complete pipeline – from data collection to simulation, generation, and editing. This offers a more holistic solution that can be immediately applied to creating a new generation of creative AI tools, media, and entertainment.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Comprehensive Framework:** Yan provides a complete pipeline for IGV, rather than focusing on a single aspect.
    *   **High Performance:** The achieved real-time, high-fidelity simulation is a major accomplishment.
    *   **Effective Combination of Techniques:** The paper leverages and adapts existing techniques (Diffusion models, VAEs, etc.) in a creative and effective way to solve a challenging problem.
    *   **Clear Results:** Qualitative results showcase the capabilities of Yan across various scenarios.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Limited Quantitative Evaluation:** The paper relies heavily on qualitative results. Quantitative metrics (e.g., user studies on the quality of interactions, quantitative comparison of simulation accuracy, benchmarking inference speed with and without optimizations) would significantly strengthen the paper.
    *   **Reliance on a Specific Game Engine:** The reliance on a specific 3D game engine (Yuan Meng Star) limits the generality of the approach.  While the authors claim generalizability, a demonstration on a different engine would be valuable.
    *   **Missing details:** Lacking important details about the exact architectural configuration makes reproducing this research harder.

*   **Potential Influence:**  Yan has the potential to be a foundational framework for IGV. The real-time performance and comprehensive capabilities could inspire future research on creative AI tools and interactive media. The hierarchical captioning and disentanglement strategies could also be adopted in other generative tasks.

**Justification:**

The paper showcases significant advancements in several areas of IGV, particularly in real-time simulation and comprehensive framework integration. The combination of novel approaches and adaptation of existing techniques justifies a relatively high score. However, the lack of quantitative results and dependence on a specific game engine limit the score from being even higher. The potential for future influence is significant.

### QoE-Aware Service Provision for Mobile AR Rendering: An Agent-Driven Approach
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08627v1)
- **Authors**: Conghao Zhou, Lulu Sun, Xiucheng Wang, Peng Yang, Feng Lyu, Sihan Lu, Xuemin Shen
- **Abstract**: Mobile augmented reality (MAR) is envisioned as a key immersive application in 6G, enabling virtual content rendering aligned with the physical environment through device pose estimation. In this paper, we propose a novel agent-driven communication service provisioning approach for edge-assisted MAR, aiming to reduce communication overhead between MAR devices and the edge server while ensuring the quality of experience (QoE). First, to address the inaccessibility of MAR application-specific information to the network controller, we establish a digital agent powered by large language models (LLMs) on behalf of the MAR service provider, bridging the data and function gap between the MAR service and network domains. Second, to cope with the user-dependent and dynamic nature of data traffic patterns for individual devices, we develop a user-level QoE modeling method that captures the relationship between communication resource demands and perceived user QoE, enabling personalized, agent-driven communication resource management. Trace-driven simulation results demonstrate that the proposed approach outperforms conventional LLM-based QoE-aware service provisioning methods in both user-level QoE modeling accuracy and communication resource efficiency.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes an agent-driven framework for QoE-aware service provisioning in edge-assisted Mobile Augmented Reality (MAR). It addresses the challenge of bridging the gap between MAR application-specific information (residing within the OTT service domain) and the network controller. The core idea is to introduce a Digital Agent (DA) that acts on behalf of the MAR service provider, leveraging a Large Language Model (LLM). This DA can access and analyze user data within the MAR service domain without exposing raw user data to the network controller. The framework utilizes a Service Function Toolkit (SFT), User Context Repository (UCR), and the DA to enable QoE-aware resource allocation. A Kalman filter-based adaptive mechanism is also incorporated to handle non-stationary user behavior. The paper presents simulation results to demonstrate the effectiveness of the proposed approach.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:**  The paper's novelty lies primarily in its specific application of LLM-powered agents to address the QoE challenges in edge-assisted MAR, specifically in the context of bridging the gap between the OTT service domain and the network controller. The use of an agent to manage application-specific information and translate it into network resource requirements is a reasonable and timely approach. While LLMs are being explored in various networking contexts, the specific architecture and integration with MAR-specific tools (SFT, UCR) show incremental innovation.  The Kalman filter for adaptive bias correction in QoE prediction also adds value.

* **Significance:** The paper tackles an important problem in the evolving landscape of 6G and immersive communications. As MAR becomes more prevalent, efficient and QoE-aware resource management will be crucial. The ability to intelligently manage network resources based on application-layer information, without compromising user privacy, is a significant step. The modular architecture comprising of the agent, SFT and UCR could potentially become a template for other cross-layer optimization problems. The performance results shown in simulation demonstrate improvement over benchmarked transformer models.

* **Strengths:**
    * The paper clearly identifies a relevant problem and presents a well-structured solution.
    * The architecture is well-defined, with a clear explanation of the roles of each component (DA, SFT, UCR).
    * The use of LLMs to bridge the domain gap is a promising approach.
    *  Simulation results provide evidence of the effectiveness of the proposed framework.
    * Integration of Kalman filter based model improves dynamic QoE assessment.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * The reliance on LLMs introduces complexity and potential issues related to latency and computational overhead. The paper doesn't fully address these practical implementation considerations.
    * The simulation setup, while using real-world head movement trajectories, is still relatively simplified.  It would be beneficial to see results in a more complex, realistic environment with varying network conditions and user behaviors.
    *  The choice of the performance baselines (BT and PT models) could be improved. There are a few other frameworks for user level MAR rendering which could have been considered as baselines.
    * The paper could provide a deeper analysis of the specific LLM prompts and tools used to achieve the desired functionality.

* **Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence future research in QoE-aware network management for immersive applications. The concept of using intelligent agents to bridge the gap between application and network layers is likely to become more widespread. The modular design could be adopted and adapted in other contexts.

**Justification for Score:**

Considering the strengths and weaknesses, the paper is scored **7**. The paper presents a relevant, novel and reasonably well-executed approach, demonstrating improvements in accuracy and efficiency. The application of LLMs to this specific MAR problem is timely and potentially impactful. However, the practicality of LLM deployment,  the lack of complexity in the simulation setup, and areas for improvement in benchmark selection detract from the overall score.

- **Score**: 7/10

### Securing Educational LLMs: A Generalised Taxonomy of Attacks on LLMs and DREAD Risk Assessment
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08629v1)
- **Authors**: Farzana Zahid, Anjalika Sewwandi, Lee Brandon, Vimal Kumar, Roopak Sinha
- **Abstract**: Due to perceptions of efficiency and significant productivity gains, various organisations, including in education, are adopting Large Language Models (LLMs) into their workflows. Educator-facing, learner-facing, and institution-facing LLMs, collectively, Educational Large Language Models (eLLMs), complement and enhance the effectiveness of teaching, learning, and academic operations. However, their integration into an educational setting raises significant cybersecurity concerns. A comprehensive landscape of contemporary attacks on LLMs and their impact on the educational environment is missing. This study presents a generalised taxonomy of fifty attacks on LLMs, which are categorized as attacks targeting either models or their infrastructure. The severity of these attacks is evaluated in the educational sector using the DREAD risk assessment framework. Our risk assessment indicates that token smuggling, adversarial prompts, direct injection, and multi-step jailbreak are critical attacks on eLLMs. The proposed taxonomy, its application in the educational environment, and our risk assessment will help academic and industrial practitioners to build resilient solutions that protect learners and institutions.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "Securing Educational LLMs: A Generalised Taxonomy of Attacks on LLMs and DREAD Risk Assessment":

**Summary:**

The paper addresses the cybersecurity concerns arising from the increasing adoption of Large Language Models (LLMs) in education. It presents a novel generalized taxonomy of 50 attacks targeting LLMs and their infrastructure. The authors categorize these attacks based on their complexity (sophistication level). The paper further quantifies the severity of these attacks within the educational context using the DREAD risk assessment framework. The risk assessment highlights critical attacks like token smuggling, adversarial prompts, direct injection, and multi-step jailbreaks. The paper aims to aid academic and industrial practitioners in building resilient solutions to protect educational institutions and learners.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The creation of a taxonomy explicitly tailored for LLM security threats, especially with a focus on the education sector, adds some novelty. The use of the DREAD risk assessment framework to prioritize these threats specifically within the education sector is also somewhat novel. However, there are existing taxonomies of LLM attacks (some mentioned in the paper's literature review), which weakens the claims of complete originality. The complexity-based categorization provides a useful perspective but doesn't necessarily introduce a fundamentally new way of thinking about these attacks compared to existing categorizations (e.g., based on attack surface).

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance lies in its attempt to bring focused attention to the security risks of LLMs within the educational sphere. Given the increasing integration of LLMs in education, a systematic analysis of potential threats is valuable. Identifying high-risk attacks like token smuggling or direct injection and mapping them to the DREAD framework provides actionable information for security practitioners in educational institutions. However, the real impact hinges on how widely the taxonomy is adopted and used in practice. The DREAD analysis, while useful, is subjective, as acknowledged by the authors, and dependent on the assessors' expertise and perspective.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   Comprehensive taxonomy: The paper identifies and categorizes a significant number of attack types.
    *   Education sector focus:  Tailoring the analysis to the education sector enhances its practical relevance.
    *   DREAD framework application: The use of the DREAD risk assessment framework offers a structured approach to prioritizing threats.
    *   Systematic Literature Review: The authors conduct a systematic literature review, indicating a thorough approach to understanding the existing landscape.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   Subjectivity in DREAD scoring:  The DREAD risk scores are inherently subjective and dependent on the assessors' perspectives. While the rationale is provided, the lack of empirical validation weakens the objectivity of the assessment.
    *   Limited empirical validation: The paper primarily focuses on taxonomy creation and risk assessment based on the reviewed literature.  It lacks any empirical validation, such as demonstrating the effectiveness of the taxonomy in real-world scenarios or the impact of the identified attacks in educational settings.
    *   Incremental contribution:  While the paper is valuable in its specific educational sector focus, the general contributions of LLM attack taxonomy and risk assessment build upon existing work in the broader field of LLM security.
    *   General Recommendations: The suggested mitigation strategies are somewhat generic (enforcing usage policies, security updates, access controls) and lack specific details on how they should be implemented in the context of the specific attacks identified in the taxonomy and the educational sector.

*   **Potential Influence:**  The paper can influence security practices in educational institutions by raising awareness of potential threats and providing a framework for assessing and prioritizing risks.  However, its influence is limited by the subjectivity of the DREAD assessment and the lack of empirical validation.

*Rigorous Rationale:* The study scores a 6 because it presents a useful and targeted taxonomy of LLM attacks in the education sector, coupled with a risk assessment using the DREAD framework. However, the work exhibits limitations, most notably subjectivity in the DREAD assessment and a lack of real-world validation. Its novelty is incremental, building upon existing LLM security literature, and its mitigation strategies are somewhat generic. A higher score would require more empirical evidence and a more fundamentally novel approach to analyzing LLM security risks.

**Score: 6**

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 6/10

### AgriGPT: a Large Language Model Ecosystem for Agriculture
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08632v1)
- **Authors**: Bo Yang, Yu Zhang, Lanfei Feng, Yunkui Chen, Jianyu Zhang, Xiao Xu, Nueraili Aierken, Yurui Li, Yuxuan Chen, Guijun Yang, Yong He, Runhe Huang, Shijian Li
- **Abstract**: Despite the rapid progress of Large Language Models (LLMs), their application in agriculture remains limited due to the lack of domain-specific models, curated datasets, and robust evaluation frameworks. To address these challenges, we propose AgriGPT, a domain-specialized LLM ecosystem for agricultural usage. At its core, we design a multi-agent scalable data engine that systematically compiles credible data sources into Agri-342K, a high-quality, standardized question-answer (QA) dataset. Trained on this dataset, AgriGPT supports a broad range of agricultural stakeholders, from practitioners to policy-makers. To enhance factual grounding, we employ Tri-RAG, a three-channel Retrieval-Augmented Generation framework combining dense retrieval, sparse retrieval, and multi-hop knowledge graph reasoning, thereby improving the LLM's reasoning reliability. For comprehensive evaluation, we introduce AgriBench-13K, a benchmark suite comprising 13 tasks with varying types and complexities. Experiments demonstrate that AgriGPT significantly outperforms general-purpose LLMs on both domain adaptation and reasoning. Beyond the model itself, AgriGPT represents a modular and extensible LLM ecosystem for agriculture, comprising structured data construction, retrieval-enhanced generation, and domain-specific evaluation. This work provides a generalizable framework for developing scientific and industry-specialized LLMs. All models, datasets, and code will be released to empower agricultural communities, especially in underserved regions, and to promote open, impactful research.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the AgriGPT paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AgriGPT, a domain-specific Large Language Model (LLM) ecosystem tailored for agricultural applications. It tackles the limited application of LLMs in agriculture due to the lack of domain-specific data, models, and evaluation benchmarks.  The AgriGPT ecosystem includes:
*   **Agri-342K Dataset:** A large, high-quality, standardized question-answer (QA) dataset created using a multi-agent scalable data engine. This dataset systematically compiles information from credible agricultural data sources.
*   **AgriGPT Model:** An LLM trained on Agri-342K, designed to support a broad range of agricultural stakeholders.
*   **Tri-RAG Framework:** A three-channel Retrieval-Augmented Generation framework (Dense, Sparse, Multi-Hop Knowledge Graph) to enhance factual grounding and reasoning reliability.
*   **AgriBench-13K Benchmark:** A suite of 13 tasks with varying types and complexities for evaluating AgriGPT.

The paper demonstrates that AgriGPT outperforms general-purpose LLMs in domain adaptation and reasoning. The authors provide modular and extensible components to promote open research and development in agriculture-specific LLMs.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The novelty lies primarily in the integration of several components to create a complete LLM ecosystem for agriculture. While domain-specific LLMs and RAG techniques exist, AgriGPT's contribution is the unified approach:
*   **Data Engine and Agri-342K:** The multi-agent data curation pipeline for creating Agri-342K appears to be a significant effort. The use of AI agents to create logically diverse and factually grounded instruction data is a notable innovation.
*   **Tri-RAG:** The combination of dense, sparse, and knowledge graph retrieval into a Tri-RAG module is a solid contribution. While the core RAG concept isn't new, the integration strategy specifically tailored for the peculiarities of agricultural data (fragmented, unstructured) does contribute to its novelty.
*   **AgriBench-13K:**  The dedicated benchmark suite is essential for assessing LLMs in this specific domain, which is beneficial for advancing models geared toward agriculture.

**Significance:** The significance of this work is high because:

*   **Addresses a Gap:** Agriculture is a vital field that has been comparatively under-represented in LLM research.
*   **Empowers Agriculture Community:** Providing accessible and open-source tools (models, datasets, benchmarks) can democratize access to AI in agriculture. This is especially impactful in underserved regions.
*   **Scalable and Transferable Framework:** The modular architecture facilitates expansion and adaptation for different agricultural needs. Its modular design facilitates transferability to other domain-specific scenarios.

**Strengths:**

*   **Comprehensive Ecosystem:** AgriGPT encompasses data creation, model training, inference optimization, and evaluation within a single framework.
*   **Open-Source:** All models, datasets, and code being released promotes accessibility and community-driven development.
*   **Demonstrated Performance:** The paper demonstrates a significant improvement in domain adaptation and reasoning compared to generic LLMs.
*   **Clear Structure and Presentation:** The paper is well-structured and clearly articulates its methodology and results.

**Weaknesses:**

*   **Dependency on DeepSeek-R1:** The data engine relies on DeepSeek-R1-671B. While this can be updated in the future, its initial reliance can be viewed as a limitation.
*   **Limited Multimodal Input:** AgriGPT currently focuses on text input. Expanding to image and sensor data is a necessary step for real-world applicability.
*   **Dialect Handling:** The model doesn't explicitly handle regional dialects, potentially limiting its usability in some areas. Future versions need to improve on capturing diverse language varieties that may occur in agriculture.

**Justification of Score:**

AgriGPT makes a solid and significant contribution to the intersection of LLMs and agriculture. The design choices are justified by the challenges inherent in agricultural data and the need for accessible, locally relevant AI solutions. The open-source nature and well-defined evaluation framework make this paper particularly valuable.

However, areas for future improvement exist. The reliance on a specific base model, the lack of multimodality, and the dialect limitation are drawbacks that temper the overall score. While novel aspects in the curated database and unique Tri-RAG setup strengthen the case, these do not fully mitigate some of the weaker aspects.

### Adaptive Personalized Conversational Information Retrieval
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08634v1)
- **Authors**: Fengran Mo, Yuchen Hui, Yuxing Tian, Zhaoxuan Tan, Chuan Meng, Zhan Su, Kaiyu Huang, Jian-Yun Nie
- **Abstract**: Personalized conversational information retrieval (CIR) systems aim to satisfy users' complex information needs through multi-turn interactions by considering user profiles. However, not all search queries require personalization. The challenge lies in appropriately incorporating personalization elements into search when needed. Most existing studies implicitly incorporate users' personal information and conversational context using large language models without distinguishing the specific requirements for each query turn. Such a ``one-size-fits-all'' personalization strategy might lead to sub-optimal results. In this paper, we propose an adaptive personalization method, in which we first identify the required personalization level for a query and integrate personalized queries with other query reformulations to produce various enhanced queries. Then, we design a personalization-aware ranking fusion approach to assign fusion weights dynamically to different reformulated queries, depending on the required personalization level. The proposed adaptive personalized conversational information retrieval framework APCIR is evaluated on two TREC iKAT datasets. The results confirm the effectiveness of adaptive personalization of APCIR by outperforming state-of-the-art methods.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper proposes "Adaptive Personalized Conversational Information Retrieval" (APCIR), a framework designed to improve personalized CIR systems by dynamically adjusting the level of personalization applied to each query turn. It addresses the challenge that not all queries in a conversation require personalization, and blindly applying personalization can degrade performance.  APCIR consists of three main components: (1) explicit personalization level identification using LLMs, (2) query reformulation based on the identified personalization level, and (3) a personalization-aware ranking fusion mechanism that dynamically assigns weights to different reformulated queries.  The framework is evaluated on TREC iKAT datasets and demonstrates improvements over existing state-of-the-art methods.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the adaptive approach to personalization.  Instead of a uniform, "one-size-fits-all" personalization strategy, the paper explicitly identifies the necessary level of personalization for each query turn (non-personalized, partially personalized, or fully personalized) and uses this to guide query reformulation and ranking.  This explicit identification step is a departure from implicit personalization techniques that rely solely on LLMs to extract relevant information from user profiles. Using LLMs to determine personalization level for CIR turns, dynamically combining them through fusion based on personalization levels, makes it a notable advancement.

**Significance:** The significance of this work stems from its potential to improve the effectiveness and efficiency of personalized CIR systems.  By avoiding unnecessary personalization, the framework aims to reduce over-personalization problems (e.g., introducing irrelevant information) and query drift. The gains demonstrated on the TREC iKAT datasets, specifically the improvement over existing state-of-the-art conversational search approaches as well as baseline fusion methods is a positive point.
The rigorous experimental evaluation on iKAT datasets, including ablation studies to analyze the contribution of each component, is a strength. The paper provides clear implementation details and makes their code available which adds to its reproducibility and impact.

**Weaknesses:** While the paper presents promising results, there are areas for further investigation.

*   **Reliance on LLMs:** While LLMs are used to identify personalization level, their reliability can still be questionable. The results shown with human intervention didn't perform well which shows the difficulty in determining the personalized relevance for different queries.
*   **Generalizability:** It is important to conduct more evaluations over diverse conversational datasets and domains to further validate the broad applicability of APCIR, since TREC iKAT is still a niche dataset.
*   **Scalability of Dynamic Weight Optimization:** the grid search employed to discover the best weights is not very efficient.
*  **Ablation on different weights**: An additional ablation in this paper is whether the weights chosen contribute to overall performance. Given the experiments involved only very limited queries, it is important to see if those weights really matter by comparing randomly selected weight to weight selection method proposed in this paper.

**Potential Influence:** The paper has the potential to influence the field of personalized CIR by highlighting the importance of adaptive personalization strategies. It encourages researchers to move beyond uniform personalization approaches and consider techniques that dynamically adjust the level of personalization based on query requirements. It introduces a structured framework that can serve as a foundation for future research in this area.

**Justification for Score:**

The paper demonstrates a sound methodology, the results are convincing, and addresses a specific challenge in personalized CIR. The focus on an adaptive approach, explicit identification of personalization levels and a ranking fusion mechanism with dynamic weights gives the paper a valuable contribution.

However, as noted earlier, several aspects would require more exploration such as reliance on LLMs and generalizability across diverse domains.

### Classifier Language Models: Unifying Sparse Finetuning and Adaptive Tokenization for Specialized Classification Tasks
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08635v1)
- **Authors**: Adit Krishnan, Chu Wang, Chris Kong
- **Abstract**: Semantic text classification requires the understanding of the contextual significance of specific tokens rather than surface-level patterns or keywords (as in rule-based or statistical text classification), making large language models (LLMs) well-suited for this task. However, semantic classification applications in industry, like customer intent detection or semantic role labeling, tend to be highly specialized. They require annotation by domain experts in contrast to general-purpose corpora for pretraining. Further, they typically require high inference throughputs which limits the model size from latency and cost perspectives. Thus, for a range of specialized classification tasks, the preferred solution is to develop customized classifiers by finetuning smaller language models (e.g., mini-encoders, small language models). In this work, we develop a token-driven sparse finetuning strategy to adapt small language models to specialized classification tasks. We identify and finetune a small sensitive subset of model parameters by leveraging task-specific token constructs in the finetuning dataset, while leaving most of the pretrained weights unchanged. Unlike adapter approaches such as low rank adaptation (LoRA), we do not introduce additional parameters to the model. Our approach identifies highly relevant semantic tokens (case study in the Appendix) and outperforms end-to-end finetuning, LoRA, layer selection, and prefix tuning on five diverse semantic classification tasks. We achieve greater stability and half the training costs vs. end-to-end finetuning.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper, along with a justified novelty score:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces AdaPT, a novel approach to adapting small language models (SLMs) for specialized semantic classification tasks. AdaPT combines task-specific tokenization with sparse finetuning to improve performance, stability, and efficiency.  It expands the model vocabulary with task-specific tokens (individual words, contiguous and non-contiguous phrases) and then uses a sensitivity metric (combining output dispersion and gradient norm) to select a small, sensitive subset of model parameters to finetune. This avoids the overfitting risks associated with end-to-end finetuning and the performance limitations of task-agnostic sparse finetuning.  The authors demonstrate the effectiveness of AdaPT across five diverse semantic classification tasks, showing that it outperforms end-to-end finetuning, LoRA, layer selection, and prefix tuning. The authors achieve greater stability, accuracy, and half the training costs compared to end-to-end finetuning.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *synergistic combination* of task-specific tokenization and sensitivity-based sparse finetuning. While both tokenization adaptation and sparse finetuning have been explored individually, AdaPT uniquely uses the *task-specific tokens to guide the selection of sensitive parameters for finetuning*.  This addresses the limitations of prior methods that either blindly finetune everything or use task-agnostic parameter selection strategies. The idea of using *gradients and output variance* as a measure of "sensitivity" is a good choice for sparse fine-tuning to reduce overfitting. It is relatively intuitive but also quite effective, as the empirical results demonstrate.
*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to make SLMs more practical for real-world semantic classification applications. By improving accuracy, stability, and efficiency, AdaPT reduces the cost and complexity of deploying custom classifiers. The demonstration of its effectiveness across diverse tasks further strengthens its practical relevance.
*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Clear problem definition and motivation:** The paper clearly articulates the challenges of adapting LLMs for specialized tasks and the limitations of existing approaches.
    *   **Well-defined approach:** AdaPT is presented in a clear and detailed manner, with algorithms outlining the token selection and finetuning processes.
    *   **Comprehensive experimental evaluation:** The authors conduct thorough experiments across five diverse tasks and compare against strong baselines, including end-to-end finetuning and other popular sparse finetuning methods.
    *   **Ablation studies:** The ablation analysis provides valuable insights into the individual contributions of task-specific tokenization and sparse finetuning.
    *   **Analysis of the selected tokens:** The case study provides valuable insights into how the method adapts to different domains, illustrating that the chosen tokens reflect the task at hand.
*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Hyperparameter Sensitivity**: The sensitivity of the method to hyperparameter choices, especially the cutoffs for perplexity and frequency in the sequence selection algorithm (Algorithm 1), is not discussed in sufficient detail. How sensitive is the accuracy or performance when the selected sequence is slightly different due to different cutoff values.
    *   **Scalability Issues**:  While the paper demonstrates effectiveness on SLMs, it does not provide convincing evidence that the approach scales to much larger LLMs. Adapting the token selection process for very large vocabularies could be a challenge. It would have been beneficial to include some discussion on the potential computational bottlenecks for very large models.
    *   **Limited Theoretical Justification**: While the theoretical discussion helps to motivate the approach, it lacks depth. Specifically, the use of Kullback-Leibler divergence or other related statistical measures to formalize the difference between the two vocabularies would further enrich the discussion.

*   **Potential Impact:** The paper has the potential to influence the development of more efficient and practical methods for adapting language models to specialized tasks. It could inspire further research into synergistic combinations of input-level and parameter-level adaptation techniques. However, the lack of large-scale validation somewhat limits the initial impact.

**Score: 7**

**Justification:**

The paper presents a novel and well-evaluated approach that effectively tackles a practical problem in semantic classification. The synergistic combination of task-specific tokenization and sparse finetuning is a key strength, leading to improved performance and efficiency. The experimental results are convincing, and the ablation analysis provides valuable insights.

However, the paper is limited by its focus on relatively small models.  While the authors make a good argument for why this is needed, there is still a need to show that this can scale to models with billions of parameters. The paper could benefit from more detailed analysis of hyperparameter sensitivity and scalability challenges. Also, it has limited discussion of related work to further distinguish this work. Therefore, while the paper represents a significant contribution, its overall impact is somewhat limited by these factors.

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### InternBootcamp Technical Report: Boosting LLM Reasoning with Verifiable Task Scaling
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08636v1)
- **Authors**: Peiji Li, Jiasheng Ye, Yongkang Chen, Yichuan Ma, Zijie Yu, Kedi Chen, Ganqu Cui, Haozhan Li, Jiacheng Chen, Chengqi Lyu, Wenwei Zhang, Linyang Li, Qipeng Guo, Dahua Lin, Bowen Zhou, Kai Chen
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) have revolutionized artificial intelligence by enabling complex reasoning capabilities. While recent advancements in reinforcement learning (RL) have primarily focused on domain-specific reasoning tasks (e.g., mathematics or code generation), real-world reasoning scenarios often require models to handle diverse and complex environments that narrow-domain benchmarks cannot fully capture. To address this gap, we present InternBootcamp, an open-source framework comprising 1000+ domain-diverse task environments specifically designed for LLM reasoning research. Our codebase offers two key functionalities: (1) automated generation of unlimited training/testing cases with configurable difficulty levels, and (2) integrated verification modules for objective response evaluation. These features make InternBootcamp fundamental infrastructure for RL-based model optimization, synthetic data generation, and model evaluation. Although manually developing such a framework with enormous task coverage is extremely cumbersome, we accelerate the development procedure through an automated agent workflow supplemented by manual validation protocols, which enables the task scope to expand rapidly. % With these bootcamps, we further establish Bootcamp-EVAL, an automatically generated benchmark for comprehensive performance assessment. Evaluation reveals that frontier models still underperform in many reasoning tasks, while training with InternBootcamp provides an effective way to significantly improve performance, leading to our 32B model that achieves state-of-the-art results on Bootcamp-EVAL and excels on other established benchmarks. In particular, we validate that consistent performance gains come from including more training tasks, namely \textbf{task scaling}, over two orders of magnitude, offering a promising route towards capable reasoning generalist.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the InternBootcamp paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces INTERNBOOTCAMP, a large-scale, open-source framework designed to improve reasoning in Large Language Models (LLMs). It consists of over 1000 diverse task environments specifically built for LLM reasoning research, spanning eight categories like algorithms, language analysis, and natural science.  The framework offers automated generation of training and testing cases with configurable difficulty levels, along with integrated verification modules for objective response evaluation. The authors create BOOTCAMP-EVAL, a benchmark for comprehensive performance assessment. Their results indicate that training with INTERNBOOTCAMP significantly improves model performance, particularly through "task scaling," where increasing the number of training tasks enhances reasoning and training efficiency. Their 32B model achieves state-of-the-art results on BOOTCAMP-EVAL and excels on other benchmarks. The code and data are publicly available.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The paper's primary novelty lies in the *scale and diversity* of the reasoning tasks provided by the INTERNBOOTCAMP framework. While existing benchmarks and environments focus on specific domains like math or code, INTERNBOOTCAMP aims for a more general reasoning capability by encompassing a broader range of tasks relevant to real-world scenarios. The automated task generation pipeline and integrated verification modules also contribute to its novelty, enabling rapid expansion of the task scope. However, components of their architecture and individual tasks have analogues in existing literature. The concept of synthesizing training data using LLMs, the RLVR approach, and the use of automated unit tests are not new in isolation. What's more, many individual tasks are adopted from existing puzzle collections and benchmarks.

*   **Significance:** The paper's significance stems from its potential to address limitations in current LLM reasoning research. By providing a diverse and scalable environment, INTERNBOOTCAMP facilitates the development of more robust and generalizable reasoning models. The task scaling experiments highlight the importance of breadth of training data for achieving better performance and efficiency in LLMs. The open-source nature of the framework also encourages further research and development in this area. The claim of superior out-of-domain (OOD) performance is compelling, but also requires a careful examination of the specific benchmarks and whether the observed improvements truly represent a generalization of reasoning capabilities, or rather task-specific tuning. It’s unclear if some of the tasks in the benchmark have already been seen by the model, since LLMs are pre-trained on vast troves of textual data.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Large-Scale and Diverse Dataset:** The framework provides a substantial number of diverse tasks.
    *   **Automated Generation & Verification:** Automates task generation and response evaluation, which are key to scale.
    *   **Task Scaling Experiment:** The empirical evidence for "task scaling" is intriguing and provides useful insights.
    *   **Open Source:** Promoting reproducibility and further community development.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Incremental Innovation:** While impressive in scope, individual components are not drastically novel compared to prior work. The paper would benefit from a clearer delineation of the specific innovations and contributions of their framework beyond its scale.
    *   **OOD Generalization Claims:** While the OOD performance is promising, a deeper analysis and more rigorous benchmarking are needed to ensure the improvements are not just overfitting to some features in the OOD benchmarks.
    *   **Automatic agent workflow** The "automated agent workflow" can use stronger reasoning models that will yield better results

*   **Impact:** The framework and benchmark have the potential to become widely used in the LLM community, especially for research focused on general reasoning and reinforcement learning. The findings on task scaling could influence the design of future training strategies for LLMs. However, the framework will likely be more impactful as a platform than as a one-off contribution. Sustained improvement and community adoption will be crucial.
    *   **Reproducibility issues:**

**Justification of Score:**

I am assigning a score of **7**.

*   It has impact on large language model community by introducing a large-scale framework INTERNBOOTCAMP.
*   I am marking down for novelty based on component innovations that are incremental and the out-of-domain generalisation claims. The incremental nature of innovation, relative to existing LLM approaches is marked down for technical issues, where reproducible issues will mark down scores.

- **Score**: 7/10

### MiGrATe: Mixed-Policy GRPO for Adaptation at Test-Time
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08641v1)
- **Authors**: Peter Phan, Dhruv Agarwal, Kavitha Srinivas, Horst Samulowitz, Pavan Kapanipathi, Andrew McCallum
- **Abstract**: Large language models (LLMs) are increasingly being applied to black-box optimization tasks, from program synthesis to molecule design. Prior work typically leverages in-context learning to iteratively guide the model towards better solutions. Such methods, however, often struggle to balance exploration of new solution spaces with exploitation of high-reward ones. Recently, test-time training (TTT) with synthetic data has shown promise in improving solution quality. However, the need for hand-crafted training data tailored to each task limits feasibility and scalability across domains. To address this problem, we introduce MiGrATe-a method for online TTT that uses GRPO as a search algorithm to adapt LLMs at inference without requiring external training data. MiGrATe operates via a mixed-policy group construction procedure that combines on-policy sampling with two off-policy data selection techniques: greedy sampling, which selects top-performing past completions, and neighborhood sampling (NS), which generates completions structurally similar to high-reward ones. Together, these components bias the policy gradient towards exploitation of promising regions in solution space, while preserving exploration through on-policy sampling. We evaluate MiGrATe on three challenging domains-word search, molecule optimization, and hypothesis+program induction on the Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC)-and find that it consistently outperforms both inference-only and TTT baselines, demonstrating the potential of online TTT as a solution for complex search tasks without external supervision.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "MIGRATE: MIXED-POLICY GRPO FOR ADAPTATION AT TEST-TIME":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces MIGRATE, a novel method for online test-time training (TTT) of Large Language Models (LLMs) to improve performance in black-box optimization tasks.  MIGRATE uses Group Relative Policy Optimization (GRPO) as a search algorithm and introduces a mixed-policy group construction strategy.  This strategy combines on-policy sampling with two off-policy techniques: greedy sampling (selecting top-performing past solutions) and neighborhood sampling (generating variations of high-reward solutions). By combining these techniques, MIGRATE aims to balance exploration and exploitation effectively without relying on external training data. The method is evaluated across three domains: word search, molecule optimization, and hypothesis+program induction on the Abstraction and Reasoning Corpus (ARC), consistently outperforming inference-only and TTT baselines.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Novelty:** The core novelty lies in the *specific combination* of existing techniques within the context of online TTT for LLM-based black-box optimization.
*   **Mixing policies:** Integrating on-policy sampling, greedy sampling, and neighborhood sampling within a GRPO framework is indeed a novel composition.  While each component has precedents (e.g., GRPO itself, neighborhood search methods), MIGRATE's synthesis is unique.
*   **Online TTT without external data:** This is a key claim, differentiating it from much of the TTT literature and off-policy RL methods that rely on offline datasets or expert demonstrations. Avoiding the need for task-specific, hand-crafted data generation is a significant contribution to practicality.

**Significance:**

*   **Practical impact:** The paper addresses a critical limitation of many LLM-based optimization approaches: the reliance on carefully designed in-context examples or training datasets. MIGRATE promises a more readily deployable solution for complex search tasks.
*   **Scalability:** Demonstrating improved performance across diverse problem domains (word search, molecule optimization, ARC) strengthens the argument for MIGRATE's general applicability. This is key for demonstrating real-world significance.
*   **Balancing Exploration and Exploitation:** The paper explicitly addresses the exploration-exploitation dilemma in LLM optimization, which is often a weak point in many approaches. MIGRATE provides a structured approach to address this.
*   **Limitations:** The paper mentions truncation due to hardware limitations which affected the ARC-Full results. This limits generalizability of the experiments as more tasks might have performed differently had they been evaluated. Furthermore, the improved numbers may not hold for tasks with longer context lengths.

**Strengths:**

*   Well-defined problem and clear motivation.
*   Novel combination of techniques within a relevant context.
*   Demonstrated empirical improvements across diverse benchmarks.
*   Addresses a practical problem (the need for external training data).

**Weaknesses:**

*   Component-wise Ablation Studies: It is not entirely clear how much the gains are attributed to the neighborhood sampling versus the greedy sampling components. Although some high-level ablation studies are performed by comparing the proposed method to using only on-policy, only greedy and only GRPO, the quantitative impact on each component should have been analyzed in more detail.

**Justification for Score:**

MIGRATE presents a solid contribution, although not a groundbreaking one. The authors have successfully combined existing techniques in a new and useful way to address a practical problem in LLM-based optimization. The empirical results are compelling, demonstrating consistent improvements across diverse domains. While the individual components of MIGRATE are not entirely novel, the specific recipe and its application to online TTT without external data offers a valuable advance. Therefore, considering the novelty of the solution, the empirical findings and the existing hardware limitations during evaluation, a score of 7 is justifiable.

**Score: 7**

- **Classification**: cs.LG
- **Score**: 7/10

### Quick on the Uptake: Eliciting Implicit Intents from Human Demonstrations for Personalized Mobile-Use Agents
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.08645v1)
- **Authors**: Zheng Wu, Heyuan Huang, Yanjia Yang, Yuanyi Song, Xingyu Lou, Weiwen Liu, Weinan Zhang, Jun Wang, Zhuosheng Zhang
- **Abstract**: As multimodal large language models advance rapidly, the automation of mobile tasks has become increasingly feasible through the use of mobile-use agents that mimic human interactions from graphical user interface. To further enhance mobile-use agents, previous studies employ demonstration learning to improve mobile-use agents from human demonstrations. However, these methods focus solely on the explicit intention flows of humans (e.g., step sequences) while neglecting implicit intention flows (e.g., personal preferences), which makes it difficult to construct personalized mobile-use agents. In this work, to evaluate the \textbf{I}ntention \textbf{A}lignment \textbf{R}ate between mobile-use agents and humans, we first collect \textbf{MobileIAR}, a dataset containing human-intent-aligned actions and ground-truth actions. This enables a comprehensive assessment of the agents' understanding of human intent. Then we propose \textbf{IFRAgent}, a framework built upon \textbf{I}ntention \textbf{F}low \textbf{R}ecognition from human demonstrations. IFRAgent analyzes explicit intention flows from human demonstrations to construct a query-level vector library of standard operating procedures (SOP), and analyzes implicit intention flows to build a user-level habit repository. IFRAgent then leverages a SOP extractor combined with retrieval-augmented generation and a query rewriter to generate personalized query and SOP from a raw ambiguous query, enhancing the alignment between mobile-use agents and human intent. Experimental results demonstrate that IFRAgent outperforms baselines by an average of 6.79\% (32.06\% relative improvement) in human intention alignment rate and improves step completion rates by an average of 5.30\% (26.34\% relative improvement). The codes are available at https://github.com/MadeAgents/Quick-on-the-Uptake.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the provided research paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Quick on the Uptake: Eliciting Implicit Intents from Human Demonstrations for Personalized Mobile-Use Agents" addresses the challenge of building personalized mobile-use agents that align with human intentions. The core idea is to go beyond simply mimicking explicit task steps (e.g., click sequences) and also capture implicit user preferences (e.g., taste preferences, habits).  The authors introduce MobileIAR, a new user-specific dataset that allows for assessment of intention alignment in mobile-use agents.  They propose a framework called IFRAgent, which analyzes both explicit and implicit intention flows extracted from human demonstrations. IFRAgent employs a standard operating procedure (SOP) extractor, retrieval-augmented generation (RAG), and a query rewriter to generate personalized queries and SOPs.  Experiments demonstrate that IFRAgent outperforms baselines in intent alignment and task completion across different mobile-use agents.

**Critical Evaluation:**

**Strengths:**

*   **Novel Problem Formulation:** The paper tackles an important and relatively underexplored aspect of mobile automation: personalized intent alignment.  While previous work focuses on task completion, this paper directly addresses the need for agents to understand and act based on user-specific preferences, habits, and ambiguous instructions. This is a critical step towards making mobile agents truly useful and user-friendly.
*   **Dataset Contribution:** The creation and release of MobileIAR are a significant contribution.
