- **Score**: 6/10

### P/D-Device: Disaggregated Large Language Model between Cloud and Devices
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09035v1)
- **Authors**: Yibo Jin, Yixu Xu, Yue Chen, Chengbin Wang, Tao Wang, Jiaqi Huang, Rongfei Zhang, Yiming Dong, Yuting Yan, Ke Cheng, Yingjie Zhu, Shulan Wang, Qianqian Tang, Shuaishuai Meng, Guanxin Cheng, Ze Wang, Shuyan Miao, Ketao Wang, Wen Liu, Yifan Yang, Tong Zhang, Anran Wang, Chengzhou Lu, Tiantian Dong, Yongsheng Zhang, Zhe Wang, Hefei Guo, Hongjie Liu, Wei Lu, Zhengyong Zhang
- **Abstract**: Serving disaggregated large language models has been widely adopted in industrial practice for enhanced performance. However, too many tokens generated in decoding phase, i.e., occupying the resources for a long time, essentially hamper the cloud from achieving a higher throughput. Meanwhile, due to limited on-device resources, the time to first token (TTFT), i.e., the latency of prefill phase, increases dramatically with the growth on prompt length. In order to concur with such a bottleneck on resources, i.e., long occupation in cloud and limited on-device computing capacity, we propose to separate large language model between cloud and devices. That is, the cloud helps a portion of the content for each device, only in its prefill phase. Specifically, after receiving the first token from the cloud, decoupling with its own prefill, the device responds to the user immediately for a lower TTFT. Then, the following tokens from cloud are presented via a speed controller for smoothed TPOT (the time per output token), until the device catches up with the progress. On-device prefill is then amortized using received tokens while the resource usage in cloud is controlled. Moreover, during cloud prefill, the prompt can be refined, using those intermediate data already generated, to further speed up on-device inference. We implement such a scheme P/D-Device, and confirm its superiority over other alternatives. We further propose an algorithm to decide the best settings. Real-trace experiments show that TTFT decreases at least 60%, maximum TPOT is about tens of milliseconds, and cloud throughput increases by up to 15x.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces P/D-Device, a novel framework for serving disaggregated large language models (LLMs) between the cloud and edge devices. The framework addresses the challenges of high Time-To-First-Token (TTFT) on devices due to limited resources and cloud throughput limitations due to long decoding phases.  P/D-Device proposes a cloud-assisted prefill stage where the cloud generates an initial set of tokens and refines the prompt. The first token is immediately sent to the device, improving TTFT. Subsequent tokens are streamed from the cloud with a smoothed TPOT (Time Per Output Token), amortizing the on-device prefill. A speed controller ensures a consistent token delivery rate. A prompt refinement technique uses intermediate cloud prefill data to generate a shorter, more relevant prompt for on-device inference. The paper includes an algorithm to determine the best settings (refinement ratio and the number of tokens assisted by the cloud), and experimental results on real-trace datasets demonstrate significant TTFT reduction, controlled TPOT, and increased cloud throughput.

**Critical Evaluation:**

* **Novelty:** The core idea of cloud-assisted prefill with prompt refinement and token streaming is reasonably novel. While disaggregated LLM serving and cloud-edge collaboration are established areas, the specific combination of these techniques, focusing on both reducing TTFT on devices and increasing cloud throughput, is a distinct contribution. The method of using cloud prefill data to refine prompts for on-device inference adds to the novelty. However, the individual components, such as prompt compression and early termination, leverage existing ideas.

* **Significance:** The paper addresses a practical problem in LLM deployment – optimizing performance in resource-constrained edge environments. The proposed P/D-Device framework offers a potential solution to balance the load between the cloud and edge. The experimental results showing a significant TTFT reduction and increased cloud throughput are valuable.  The framework could enable broader adoption of LLMs on devices, benefiting user experience and system scalability. The adaptive decision mechanism makes the proposed framework have good practical values.

* **Strengths:**
    * **Well-defined problem:** The paper clearly identifies the challenges of deploying LLMs on devices and cloud serving.
    * **Comprehensive approach:**  P/D-Device addresses both TTFT and throughput limitations through a combination of techniques.
    * **Prompt Refinement:** Leveraging cloud prefill to refine prompts is a novel approach to improve on-device inference.
    * **Experimental validation:** The paper presents results on real-trace datasets, demonstrating the effectiveness of the framework.
    * **Adaptive algorithm:** This provides the framework more practicality.

* **Weaknesses:**
    * **Reliance on Existing Techniques:**  The framework heavily relies on existing techniques like prompt compression, speculative decoding, and early termination. While the integration is novel, the individual contributions of these components are not always clearly delineated.
    * **Complexity:** The framework introduces several parameters and configurations, potentially increasing the complexity of deployment and optimization.  A more detailed discussion of the practical considerations for tuning these parameters would be helpful.
    * **Limited Quality Evaluation:** While the quality results are good, there could be more in-depth quality evaluation, such as comprehensive comparison on output text. The dataset and evaluation metrics could be improved.
    * **Lack of comparison on training efficiency:** Currently the paper mainly focuses on the inference efficiency. A comparison on the training phase would be better.

* **Potential Influence:** P/D-Device has the potential to influence the design of future cloud-edge collaborative LLM serving systems.  The prompt refinement and token streaming techniques could be adopted in other frameworks.  The paper could also motivate further research on adaptive resource allocation and load balancing in disaggregated LLM serving environments.

* **Justification for Score:** Considering the reasonable novelty in integrating existing techniques into a comprehensive framework, the significance of the work in addressing a practical problem, the strengths of the experimental evaluation, and the limitations in reliance on existing techniques, quality evaluation, a score of 7 is assigned.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.DC
- **Score**: 7/10

### Can We Trust AI to Govern AI? Benchmarking LLM Performance on Privacy and AI Governance Exams
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09036v1)
- **Authors**: Zane Witherspoon, Thet Mon Aye, YingYing Hao
- **Abstract**: The rapid emergence of large language models (LLMs) has raised urgent questions across the modern workforce about this new technology's strengths, weaknesses, and capabilities. For privacy professionals, the question is whether these AI systems can provide reliable support on regulatory compliance, privacy program management, and AI governance. In this study, we evaluate ten leading open and closed LLMs, including models from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta, and DeepSeek, by benchmarking their performance on industry-standard certification exams: CIPP/US, CIPM, CIPT, and AIGP from the International Association of Privacy Professionals (IAPP). Each model was tested using official sample exams in a closed-book setting and compared to IAPP's passing thresholds. Our findings show that several frontier models such as Gemini 2.5 Pro and OpenAI's GPT-5 consistently achieve scores exceeding the standards for professional human certification - demonstrating substantial expertise in privacy law, technical controls, and AI governance. The results highlight both the strengths and domain-specific gaps of current LLMs and offer practical insights for privacy officers, compliance leads, and technologists assessing the readiness of AI tools for high-stakes data governance roles. This paper provides an overview for professionals navigating the intersection of AI advancement and regulatory risk and establishes a machine benchmark based on human-centric evaluations.
- **Summary**: Here is a summary and critical evaluation of the provided paper:

**Summary:**

The paper "Can We Trust AI to Govern AI? Benchmarking LLM Performance on Privacy and AI Governance Exams" explores the capability of large language models (LLMs) to handle tasks related to privacy and AI governance. It benchmarks ten leading LLMs, including those from OpenAI, Anthropic, Google DeepMind, Meta, and DeepSeek, by testing their performance on industry-standard certification exams (CIPP/US, CIPM, CIPT, and AIGP) from the International Association of Privacy Professionals (IAPP). The models were tested in a closed-book setting using official sample exams, and their scores were compared to IAPP's passing thresholds. The results show that some frontier models like Gemini 2.5 Pro and OpenAI's GPT-5 consistently achieve scores exceeding human certification standards, demonstrating substantial expertise in privacy law, technical controls, and AI governance. The paper analyzes both the strengths and weaknesses of current LLMs, offering insights for professionals assessing the readiness of AI tools for data governance roles.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Practical Relevance:** The paper addresses a highly relevant and pressing question: the potential of AI in governing AI, particularly concerning privacy and regulatory compliance. This is crucial given the increasing reliance on AI and the complexity of data governance.
    *   **Rigorous Methodology:** Using industry-standard certification exams as benchmarks provides a solid and quantifiable method to assess LLM performance. The closed-book setting and adherence to exam scoring standards ensure a fair comparison against human benchmarks.
    *   **Comprehensive Model Evaluation:** The study evaluates a diverse range of LLMs, including both open and closed-source models, allowing for a comparative analysis of different approaches.
    *   **In-depth Analysis:** The paper goes beyond simple scoring, analyzing performance across different exam subdomains to identify specific strengths and weaknesses of the models. It also explores correlations between performance on different exams.
    *   **Actionable Insights:** The results offer practical insights for privacy officers, compliance leads, and technologists by identifying suitable AI tools for data governance roles and highlighting areas where LLMs need further improvement.
    *   **Novelty:** Applying standardized professional exams to evaluate LLM competence in a regulated domain like AI governance is a novel approach and presents a quantifiable benchmark.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Sample Exams vs. Real Exams:** The study uses sample exams, which may not fully represent the difficulty and nuances of the actual certification exams. This could lead to an overestimation of the models' true capabilities.
    *   **Prompt Sensitivity:** As the authors acknowledge, prompt engineering can significantly impact LLM performance. Although prompts were standardized, there's still a possibility that variations in prompt wording or framing could influence results. The "removing" of the ranking question and substituting it with a standard multiple choice may have introduced bias.
    *   **Limited Scope:** While the chosen IAPP certifications are well-regarded, they represent a specific perspective on privacy and AI governance. Other certifications or regulatory frameworks might yield different results.
    *   **Generalizability:** The findings are primarily focused on the US legal framework. Performance on similar exams for other jurisdictions (e.g., GDPR in Europe) could vary significantly.
    *   **Lack of qualitative error analysis**: There could be a qualitative error analysis performed on the cases where LLMs do not perform well to pinpoint if they make egregious legal errors, or if they perform poorly because of other reasons.
    *   **Lack of statistical significance tests.** The performance of all LLMs would ideally have statistical significance tests done to compare the performance of the different LLMs.

*   **Significance and Impact:**

    *   The paper has the potential to significantly influence how AI tools are evaluated and deployed in the privacy and AI governance sectors.
    *   It provides a valuable benchmark for future LLM development by establishing a quantifiable measure of expertise in these critical areas.
    *   The insights can inform the development of more specialized and effective AI tools for data governance and regulatory compliance.
    *   The work can also spur further research into the capabilities and limitations of LLMs in high-stakes, regulated domains.

**Justification of the Score:**

The paper makes a valuable contribution by applying a novel approach to benchmark LLM performance in a practically relevant and high-stakes domain. The methodology is well-designed and the analysis is thorough. The paper can be criticized however, because it does not analyze qualitatively if they perform egregious legal errors or not, as well as does not provide statistical significance tests to properly compare the different LLM models. The weaknesses primarily relate to the use of sample exams, a potential dependence on prompt design, and a focus on a specific set of certifications. However, given the significance of the problem and the rigor of the evaluation, a score of 7 is justified. It's a solid, impactful piece of work with clear implications for the future of AI in governance, but it has areas for improvement that would raise its score higher.

Score: 7

- **Classification**: cs.CY
- **Score**: 7/10

### LLM-as-a-Supervisor: Mistaken Therapeutic Behaviors Trigger Targeted Supervisory Feedback
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09042v1)
- **Authors**: Chen Xu, Zhenyu Lv, Tian Lan, Xianyang Wang, Luyao Ji, Leyang Cui, Minqiang Yang, Jian Shen, Qunxi Dong, Xiuling Liu, Juan Wang, Bin Hu
- **Abstract**: Although large language models (LLMs) hold significant promise in psychotherapy, their direct application in patient-facing scenarios raises ethical and safety concerns. Therefore, this work shifts towards developing an LLM as a supervisor to train real therapists. In addition to the privacy of clinical therapist training data, a fundamental contradiction complicates the training of therapeutic behaviors: clear feedback standards are necessary to ensure a controlled training system, yet there is no absolute "gold standard" for appropriate therapeutic behaviors in practice. In contrast, many common therapeutic mistakes are universal and identifiable, making them effective triggers for targeted feedback that can serve as clearer evidence. Motivated by this, we create a novel therapist-training paradigm: (1) guidelines for mistaken behaviors and targeted correction strategies are first established as standards; (2) a human-in-the-loop dialogue-feedback dataset is then constructed, where a mistake-prone agent intentionally makes standard mistakes during interviews naturally, and a supervisor agent locates and identifies mistakes and provides targeted feedback; (3) after fine-tuning on this dataset, the final supervisor model is provided for real therapist training. The detailed experimental results of automated, human and downstream assessments demonstrate that models fine-tuned on our dataset MATE, can provide high-quality feedback according to the clinical guideline, showing significant potential for the therapist training scenario.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper:

**Summary:**

The paper introduces a novel approach to training therapists by using large language models (LLMs) as supervisors. Instead of directly deploying LLMs for patient-facing therapy (which has ethical and practical concerns), the authors focus on training *real* therapists. They tackle the challenge that "gold standard" therapeutic behaviors are hard to define. Their approach centers on identifying common *mistakes* made by therapists, using these as triggers for targeted supervisory feedback.  They create a dataset called MATE (Mistaken behaviors to TargEted feedback) built on a multi-agent framework: a mistake-prone therapist agent, a client agent, and a supervisor agent, with human-in-the-loop oversight. The supervisor LLM is then fine-tuned on this dataset. The paper demonstrates that this LLM can effectively identify mistake locations, categorize them, and provide corrective feedback aligned with clinical guidelines. The evaluations show performance improvements across multiple objective metrics, subjective evaluations, and downstream tasks (like empathy classification), and increased self-efficacy in participating novice therapists.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The approach is indeed novel. The shift from LLM-as-therapist to LLM-as-supervisor is important, sidestepping some immediate ethical concerns. The focus on "mistakes" rather than a potentially ambiguous definition of "good therapy" provides a clearer training signal. The multi-agent framework for dataset creation is a unique and controlled way to generate training data, given the scarcity of labeled clinical data. PATIENT-Ψ-TRAINER also looks at LLMs as assistant tools, and this goes a step further than that.

*   **Significance:**  The work has the potential to significantly impact therapist training. The approach addresses the real-world problem of therapist shortages and limitations in access to high-quality supervision. By providing targeted feedback on common mistakes, it may accelerate the development of therapists. This kind of approach is likely more scalable than relying solely on human supervisors. The demonstrated improvements on objective measures (mistake identification) are significant, which are crucial components for future adoption of these methods for therapist training.

*   **Strengths:**
    *   **Problem Framing:**  The paper clearly identifies a critical problem in mental healthcare.
    *   **Ethical Considerations:** The chosen task avoids the significant ethical issues associated with LLM-as-therapist.
    *   **Methodological Rigor:** The paper employs a systematic and multi-faceted approach, including both objective and subjective evaluations. The human-in-the-loop design of the dataset creation process adds to the credibility of the dataset.
    *   **Empirical Validation:** Strong empirical results are shown in the paper supporting the claim of increased counselor self-efficacy and better quality feedback.
    *   **Focus on Mistake-Triggered Feedback:** Training on mistakes is a powerful approach to ensuring robust adherence to some best-practice guidelines.

*   **Weaknesses:**
    *   **Dataset Scope:** While the MATE dataset is valuable, the paper also mentions it incorporates 16 total patterns of therapy mistakes. This number, while non-negligible, is relatively limited in the context of more complex therapeutic interventions, requiring more expansive datasets. It is important to note that the models’ comprehensive capabilities may be limited by the scale of the dataset.
    *   **Generalizability:** The study involves a relatively small sample of psychology graduate students, it is vital that future researchers validate these findings with larger, more diverse cohorts to ensure broad applicability.
    *   **Complexity:** There is inherent complexity with relying on LLMs for human intervention. This can lead to errors or mistakes, that can have implications if this model were used in practice.
    *   **No Clinical Validation:** Clinical use validation would significantly strengthen the paper.

*   **Potential Influence:** This research could significantly influence the development of AI-assisted tools for therapist training. It provides a concrete and well-validated approach that others can build upon. The emphasis on avoiding direct patient interaction makes the approach more likely to be adopted in clinical settings. There is room for future work to validate the clinical utility of the approach in supporting therapists for patient care, and its utility with diverse patient groups and conditions.

**Score: 8**

**Rationale:** The paper introduces a genuinely novel approach to a practically important problem. The multi-agent data generation and the focus on mistakes are significant contributions. The evaluations are thorough and demonstrate the effectiveness of the approach. While the size and scope of the dataset are limited, this is a good starting point that holds considerable promise. Clinical validation, as mentioned in weaknesses, would push this paper into the 9-10 range. Overall, this is a strong and influential contribution, justifying the high score.

- **Classification**: cs.CL
- **Score**: 8/10

### READER: Retrieval-Assisted Drafter for Efficient LLM Inference
- **Link**: [Link to Paper](http://arxiv.org/abs/2508.09072v1)
- **Authors**: Maxim Divilkovskiy, Vitaly Malygin, Sergey Zlobin, Sultan Isali, Vasily Kalugin, Stanislav Ilyushin, Nuriza Aitassova, Yi Fei, Zeng Weidi
- **Abstract**: Large Language Models (LLMs) generate tokens autoregressively, with each token depending on the preceding context. This sequential nature makes the inference process inherently difficult to accelerate, posing a significant challenge for efficient deployment. In recent years, various methods have been proposed to address this issue, with the most effective approaches often involving the training of additional draft models. In this paper, we introduce READER (Retrieval-Assisted Drafter for Efficient LLM Inference), a novel lossless speculative decoding method that enhances model-based approaches by leveraging self-repetitions in the text. Our algorithm expands the speculative decoding tree using tokens obtained through statistical search. This work focuses on large batch sizes (>= 8), an underexplored yet important area for industrial applications. We also analyze the key-value (KV) cache size during speculative decoding and propose an optimization to improve performance for large batches. As a result, READER outperforms existing speculative decoding methods. Notably, READER requires no additional training and can reuse pre-trained speculator models, increasing the speedup by over 40\%. Our method demonstrates particularly strong performance on search-based tasks, such as retrieval-augmented generation, where we achieve more than 10x speedup.
- **Summary**: Here's a summary and critical evaluation of the paper "READER: Retrieval-Assisted Drafter for Efficient LLM Inference":

**Summary:**

The paper introduces READER, a novel lossless speculative decoding method designed to enhance the efficiency of large language model (LLM) inference, particularly for large batch sizes (≥ 8).  READER leverages self-repetitions within text by expanding the speculative decoding tree using tokens obtained through statistical search (using tries and suffix arrays against a datastore).  The method reuses existing pre-trained speculator models and combines them with a statistically derived tree branch. The authors propose a key-value (KV) cache optimization to improve performance, especially with large batches. Experiments demonstrate that READER outperforms existing speculative decoding methods, achieving significant speedups (over 40%, and more than 10x for retrieval-augmented generation tasks) without requiring additional training.

**Critical Evaluation:**

*   **Novelty:** The core idea of combining model-based speculative decoding with statistical search (retrieval of repeating sequences) has some novelty. While several prior works use either draft models or statistical search independently, the systematic combination of these techniques, particularly with the specific datastore and tree-merging approach described in READER, is a worthwhile innovation. The KV cache rearrangement strategy also adds a practical optimization layer. The idea of only adding one branch from the statistically generated tree into the pre-existing draft tree to facilitate fixed batch sizes, makes it more practical.
*   **Significance:** The significance lies in its potential to improve LLM inference efficiency, a crucial factor for wider deployment and accessibility. The gains reported (40% speedup overall, >10x for RAG) are substantial and practically relevant. The fact that READER requires no additional training of the draft model increases its appeal and ease of adoption. The analysis of KV cache bottlenecks and the proposed solution address a real-world constraint. However, the extent to which this can generalize to various LLMs or hardware is not clear.

*   **Strengths:**

    *   **Strong Experimental Results:** The experimental results are compelling, showcasing significant speedups on diverse tasks and datasets. The ablation study helps isolate the impact of each component of READER.
    *   **Practicality:** The method is presented as training-free, meaning it reuses existing speculative models, which is valuable for deployment.
    *   **Addresses a Real Problem:** LLM inference is computationally expensive, and READER tackles this directly, making LLMs more usable.
    *   **KV Cache Optimization:** Recognizes and addresses a critical bottleneck in LLM inference, contributing to the overall efficiency.

*   **Weaknesses:**

    *   **Limited Generalizability Discussion:** While the experiments cover various models and tasks, the paper lacks a deeper analysis of the conditions under which READER is most effective. The performance might depend heavily on the specific characteristics of the dataset and the frequency of self-repetitions. The paper has very little theory and explanation about why it worked well.
    *   **Parameter Tuning:** There is a discussion around tree depth, however, it is not exhaustive in the methods used to arrive at those optimal parameters.
    *   **Datastore Dependence:** The method relies on a pre-built datastore for statistical search. The construction and maintenance of this datastore, as well as its impact on memory footprint, are not thoroughly addressed. What is the best way to build this datastore? What is the trade-off of its size versus performance?
    *   **Reproducibility:** Although details are provided, the level of engineering to produce the software makes full reproduction difficult.

*   **Potential Influence:** READER has the potential to influence future research on LLM inference and to inspire other methods that combine model-based and retrieval-based techniques. The focus on large batch sizes is particularly valuable. Further investigation into the datastore construction and optimization would be beneficial to the field.

**Justification for Score:**

I assign a score of 7.

*   The paper presents a novel combination of existing techniques (model-based speculative decoding and statistical search), resulting in a practical and efficient inference method.
*   The experimental results are strong, demonstrating significant speedups on diverse tasks.
*   The method's training-free nature is a significant advantage for deployment.
*   However, the paper could benefit from a more in-depth analysis of the method's generalizability, datastore optimization, and potential limitations. There is a lack of discussion regarding theoretical foundations.
